<div><tool-result call-id="toolu_017ANq4rvE33zkKCuzNazPks"><script type="application/json">{"version":1,"status":"success","result":{"type":"text","value":"[File: 51.8KB, 1838 lines, showing lines 1-1838]\n     1→---\r\n     2→title: \"機率與單變量分配\"\r\n   ...[truncated, total 63652 chars]"},"resultPreview":{"type":"code","value":"[File: 51.8KB, 1838 lines, showing lines 1-1838]\n---\rtitle: \"機率與單變量分配\"\r---\r\r::: {.callout-tip title=\"本章定位\"}\r本講義由蘇南誠老師編授。感謝黃怡婷老師提供原始數理統計講義檔案；本章保留原有英文術語、公式與例題，並整理為可連續閱讀的教學形式。\r\r學生在大二已使用 Hogg, Tanis & Zimmerman 的 *Probability and Statistical Inference* 學過機率基礎，因此本章**不是重新教授機率論**，而是採取\r\r$$\r\\boxed{\\text{Recall}\\longrightarrow\\text{Deepen}\\longrightarrow\\text{Connect}}\r$$\r\r的三階段安排：先快速喚醒已學內容，再深化數理統計所需的推導工具，最後連接抽樣分配、收斂與後續推論。標題保留 Hogg, McKean & Craig Chapter 1 的 section numbering，方便對照課本；各節不採等比例講解。\r:::\r\r## 學習目標\r\r完成本章後，學生應能：\r\r- 以樣本空間與事件描述隨機實驗\r- 運用條件機率、全機率公式與 Bayes 定理\r- 辨認離散與連續隨機變數的分配\r- 計算期望、變異數、動差與動差生成函數\r- 使用 Markov、Chebyshev 與 Jensen 不等式\r- 由 Chebyshev 不等式推導樣本平均數的依機率收斂\r\r## 必備的先備知識\r\r集合符號、基本微積分與有限和。機率法則、條件機率、隨機變數及 pmf / pdf / cdf 視為大二已學內容，課堂中以診斷題與例題快速喚醒。\r\r::: {.callout-note title=\"教學節奏\"}\r- **Stage 1 — Recall（快速）**：Sections 1.1--1.5，以 60--90 分鐘的 example-driven review 喚醒基本概念；忘記的內容在例題中補回，不重走完整的 Definition--Theorem--Proof 流程。\r- **Stage 2 — Deepen（主體）**：Sections 1.6--1.9。1.6 與 1.7 的 pmf / pdf 基礎快速帶過，把時間放在 transformation；1.8 與 1.9 深入處理 $E[g(X)]$、moments、variance 與 MGF 的推導。\r- **Stage 3 — Connect（橋接）**：Section 1.10。以重要不等式連接 sample mean、Chebyshev、convergence in probability 與後續 mathematical statistics。\r:::\r\r## Stage 1 — Recall：快速喚醒 {.unnumbered}\r\r> **學過的，快速喚醒；忘記的，在例題中補回來。**\r\r本階段以概念辨識與短例題為主。學生應能迅速回答 $P(A\\mid B)$、$F_X(x)=P(X\\le x)$、pmf / pdf 的差異及 cdf 的基本性質；細部證明可依診斷結果選講。\r\r## 1.1 Introduction\r\r### Definition of experiment\r\r- Random experiments\r\r  - Outcome cannot be predicted with certainty.\r\r### Sample space\r\r- Outcome (Sample) space\r\r  - Is the collection of all possible outcomes.\r\r  - Is known.\r\r  - Is denoted as $\\mathcal{S}$.\r\r### Event\r\r- Event\r\r  - Is a part of the collection of outcomes in $\\mathcal{S}$.\r\r  - Is denoted as $A$.\r\r  - $A\\subset \\mathcal{S}$.\r\r### Frequency and relative frequency\r\r- The probability of an event $A$ is called the chance of $A$ occurring.\r\r- For example, a fair coin is tossed repeatedly $n$ times.\r\r- Count the number of times that event $A$ (Head) occurs.\r\r- The number is called frequency and is denoted as $N(A)$.\r\r- The relative frequency is $N(A)/n$.\r\r  - $n=10$\r\r  - H T H H T T H H T H\r\r  - H: 6/10; T: 4/10\r\r### Features of relative frequency\r\r- As $n$ tends to $\\infty$, relative frequency tends to be a fixed value.\r\r- Suppose this fixed value is $p$.\r\r- $p$ is considered as the future performance of outcomes (event $A$) of the experiment.\r\r- Tossing coin experiment $n$ times\r\r  - $S=\\{H, T\\}$\r\r  - $N(S) = N(H) + N(T) = n$\r\r  - $N(S)/n =1$\r\r## 1.2 Sets\r\r### Algebra of sets\r\r- Null or empty set\r\r  - $\\emptyset$\r\r- Subset\r\r  - $A\\subset B$\r\r- Intersection\r\r  - $A\\cap B$\r\r- Union\r\r  - $A\\cup B$\r\r- Complement\r\r  - $A^{C}$ or $A^{'}$\r\r### Venn Diagrams\r\r+:------------------------------------:+:------------------------------------:+\r| ![](assets/graph/fig1-1-1a.png) | ![](assets/graph/fig1-1-1b.png) |\r+--------------------------------------+--------------------------------------+\r| ![](assets/graph/fig1-1-1c.png)                                        |\r+-----------------------------------------------------------------------------+\r\r### Properties for set operations\r\r- Commutative law $$\\begin{aligned}\r  A \\cup B &= B \\cup A\\\\\r  A \\cap B &= B \\cap A\r  \\end{aligned}$$\r\r- Associative law $$\\begin{aligned}\r  (A \\cup B) \\cup C &= A \\cup (B \\cup C)\\\\\r  (A \\cap B) \\cap C &= A \\cap (B \\cap C)\r  \\end{aligned}$$\r\r### Properties for set operations\r\r- Distributive law $$\\begin{aligned}\r  A \\cap (B \\cup C) &= (A \\cap B) \\cup (A \\cap C)\\\\\r  A \\cup (B \\cap C) &= (A \\cup B) \\cap (A \\cup C)\\\\\r  \\end{aligned}$$\r\r- De Morgan's law $$\\begin{aligned}\r  (A \\cup B)^{'} &= A^{'} \\cap B^{'}\\\\\r  (A \\cap B) ^{'} &= A^{'} \\cup B^{'}\\\\\r  \\end{aligned}$$\r\r### Venn Diagrams\r\r  -------------------------------------- --------------------------------------\r   ![](assets/graph/fig1-1-2a.png)   ![](assets/graph/fig1-1-2b.png)\r  -------------------------------------- --------------------------------------\r\r### More than 2 sets\r\r- Let $A_1,A_2,\\ldots, A_n$ be any sets.\r\r- Define\r\r  - $A_1 \\cup A_2 \\cup \\cdots \\cup A_n$ = $\\{x: x\\in A_i,~\\text{for some }i=1, 2, \\ldots, n\\}$ = $\\cup_{i=1}^n A_i$.\r\r  - $A_1 \\cap A_2 \\cap \\cdots \\cap A_n$ = $\\{x: x\\in A_i,~\\text{for all }i=1, 2, \\ldots, n\\}$ = $\\cap_{i=1}^n A_i$.\r\r### Countable sets\r\r- Let $A_1,A_2,\\ldots, A_n, \\ldots$ be a sequence of sets.\r\r- Define\r\r  - $A_1 \\cup A_2 \\cup \\cdots \\cup A_n \\cup \\cdots$ = $\\{x: x\\in A_i,~\\text{for some }i = 1, 2, \\ldots, n, \\ldots\\}$ = $\\cup_{n=1}^\\infty A_n$.\r\r  - $A_1 \\cap A_2 \\cap \\cdots \\cap A_n \\cap \\cdots$ = $\\{x: x\\in A_i,~\\text{for all }i=1, 2, \\ldots, n, \\ldots\\}$ = $\\cap_{n=1}^\\infty A_n$.\r\r### Example 1\r\r- Let $\\mathcal{S}$ = {1, 2, 3, $\\ldots$}.\r\r- Let $A_n = \\{1, 3, \\ldots, 2n-1\\}$ and $B_n$ = {$n, n+1, \\ldots,$} for $n=1, 2, 3, \\ldots$.\r\r- Find $\\cup_{n=1}^\\infty A_n$, $\\cap_{n=1}^\\infty A_n$, $\\cup_{n=1}^\\infty B_n$, $\\cap_{n=1}^\\infty B_n$.\r\r### Example 2\r\r- Let $\\mathcal{C}$ = (0, 5).\r\r- Let $C_n$ = $(1-n^{-1}, 2+n^{-1})$ and $D_n$ = $(n^{-1}, 3-n^{-1})$, $n = 1, 2, 3, \\ldots$.\r\r- Find $\\cup_{n=1}^\\infty C_n$, $\\cap_{n=1}^\\infty C_n$, $\\cup_{n=1}^\\infty D_n$, $\\cap_{n=1}^\\infty D_n$.\r\r### Monotone (nondecreasing) sets\r\r- Let $A_1,A_2,\\ldots, A_n, \\ldots$ be a sequence of nondecreasing sets, i.e., $$A_n \\subset A_{n+1}, n = 1, 2, 3, \\ldots$$\r\r- Define $$\\lim_{n\\rightarrow \\infty} A_n = \\cup_{n=1}^\\infty A_n.$$\r\r### Monotone (nonincreasing) sets\r\r- Let $A_1,A_2,\\ldots, A_n, \\ldots$ be a sequence of nonincreasing sets, i.e., $$A_n \\supset A_{n+1}, n = 1, 2, 3, \\ldots$$\r\r- Define $$\\lim_{n\\rightarrow \\infty} A_n = \\cap_{n=1}^\\infty A_n.$$\r\r### Set functions\r\r- Set function（集合函數）\r\r  - Is a function that map sets into real numbers.\r\r### Example 3\r\r- Let ${\\mathcal{S}}$ = $R$ be a set of real numbers.\r\r- Consider a subset $A\\in \\mathcal{S}$.\r\r- Let $Q(A)$ be a set function of $A$ and be equal to = {the number of points in $A$ that correspond to positive integers}.\r\r- If $A = \\{x: 0\u003c x\u003c 5\\}$, find $Q(A)$.\r\r- If $A = \\{x: -2\u003c x\u003c -1\\}$, find $Q(A)$.\r\r- If $A = \\{x: -\\infty \u003c x\u003c 6\\}$, find $Q(A)$.\r\r### Example 4\r\r- Let ${\\mathcal{S}}$ = $R^2$ be a set of real numbers.\r\r- Consider a subset $A\\in \\mathcal{S}$.\r\r- Let $Q(A)$ be a set function of $A$ and be equal to = {the area of $A$} if $A$ has a finite area; otherwise, be undefined.\r\r- If $A = \\{(x, y): x^2 + y^2 \\le 1\\}$, find $Q(A)$.\r\r- If $A = \\{(0, 0), (1, 1), (0, 1)\\}$, find $Q(A)$.\r\r- If $A = \\{(x, y): 0\\le x, 0\\le y, x+y\\le 1\\}$, find $Q(A)$.\r\r### Common set functions\r\r- Suppose $\\mathcal{S}$ be a set of real number.\r\r  - $Q(A) = \\int_A f(x) dx$\r\r  - $Q(A) = \\int\\int_A g(x, y) dxdy$\r\r- Suppose $\\mathcal{C}$ be a set of integers.\r\r  - $Q(A) = \\sum_{x\\in A} f(x)$\r\r  - $Q(A) = \\sum\\sum_{(x, y) \\in A} g(x, y)$\r\r### Example 5\r\r- Suppose $\\mathcal{S}$ be a set of all nonnegative integers.\r\r- Let $A$ be a subset of $\\mathcal{S}$.\r\r- Define\r\r  - $Q(A) = \\sum_{n\\in A} \\left( \\frac{2}{3}\\right)^n$\r\r- Find $Q($$)$.\r\r- If $A = \\{1, 2, 3\\}$, find $Q(A)$.\r\r- If $B = \\{1, 3, 5, \\ldots\\}$ is the set of all odd positive integers, find $Q(B)$.\r\r### Example 6\r\r- Suppose $\\mathcal{S}$ be the interval of positive real numbers, i.e., $\\mathcal{S}$ = $(0, \\infty)$.\r\r- Let $A$ be a subset of $\\mathcal{S}$.\r\r- Define\r\r  - $Q(A) = \\int_A e^{-x}dx$, provided the integral exists.\r\r- Find $Q((1, 3))$.\r\r- Find $Q((5, \\infty))$.\r\r- Find $Q((1, 3) \\cup [3, 5))$.\r\r- Find $Q(\\mathcal{S})$.\r\r### Example 7\r\r- Let ${\\mathcal{S}}$ = $R^n$.\r\r- Let $A = \\{(x_1, x_2, \\ldots, x_n): 0\\le x_1\\le x_2, 0\\le x_i\\le 1, \\text{for }i = 1, 2, \\ldots, n\\}$.\r\r- Let $B = \\{(x_1, x_2, \\ldots, x_n): 0\\le x_1\\le x_2\u003c \\cdots \\le x_n \\le 1\\}$.\r\r- Find $Q(A)$ and $Q(B)$.\r\r## 1.3 Probability Set Function\r\r### Recall frequency\r\r- The probability of an event $A$ equals $p$.\r\r- Denote as $P[A]$.\r\r- $P[A]$ is a set function that associates $A$ with the relative frequency.\r\r### Definition of Probability\r\r::: {#def-ch1-1}\r- Let $\\mathcal{S}$ be a sample space.\r\r- Let $\\mathcal{B}$ be the set of events.\r\r- Let $P$ be a real-valued set function defined on $\\mathcal{B}$.\r\r- $P$ is a probability set function if it satisfies\r\r  - $P[A]\\ge 0$, for all $A\\in \\mathcal{B}$.\r\r  - $P[$$] = 1$\r\r  - If $\\{A_n\\}$ is a sequence of events in $\\mathcal{B}$ and $A_m\\cap A_n =\\emptyset$ for all $m\\ne n$, then $$P[A_1\\cup A_2 \\cup A_3 \\cup \\cdots ] = \\sum_{i=1}^{\\infty} P[A_i]$$\r:::\r\r### Special terminology\r\r- Mutually exclusive events\r\r  - $A_i \\cap A_j = \\emptyset$, $\\forall i\\ne j$ .\r\r  - $A_1, \\cdots, A_k$ are disjoint sets.\r\r  - The union is referred to as a disjoint union.\r\r- Exhaustive events, if\r\r  - $A_i \\cap A_j = \\emptyset$, $\\forall i\\ne j$ .\r\r  - $\\cup_{n=1}^{\\infty} A_n = \\mathcal{S}$.\r\r### Special terminology\r\r- Mutually exclusive and exhaustive events\r\r  - $A_i \\cap A_j = \\emptyset$, $\\forall i\\ne j$.\r\r  - $A_1, \\cdots, A_k$ are disjoint sets.\r\r  - $A_1\\cup A_2 \\cup \\cdots A_k =S$.\r\r- It is a partition of ${\\mathcal{S}}$.\r\r### Theorem\r\r::: {#thm-ch1-1}\rFor each event $A \\in {\\mathcal{B}}$, $$P[A]=1-P[A^{'}]$$\r:::\r\r### Theorem\r\r::: {#thm-ch1-2}\r$$P[\\emptyset]=0$$\r:::\r\r### Theorem\r\r::: {#thm-ch1-3}\rIf events $A$ and $B$ are such that $A\\subset B$, then $$P[A]\\le P[B].$$\r:::\r\r### Theorem\r\r::: {#thm-ch1-4}\rFor each event $A \\in {\\mathcal{B}}$, $$P[A]\\le 1.$$\r:::\r\r### Theorem\r\r::: {#thm-ch1-5}\rIf $A$ and $B$ are any two events in ${\\mathcal{S}}$, then $$P[A\\cup B]= P[A]+P[B]-P[A\\cap B].$$\r:::\r\r### Theorem\r\r::: {#thm-ch1-6}\rIf $A$, $B$ and $C$ are any three events, then $$\\begin{aligned}\rP[A\\cup B\\cup C]=& P[A]+P[B]+P[C]-P[A\\cap B]-P[A\\cap C]\\\\\r&-P[B\\cap C]+P[A\\cap B\\cap C].\r\\end{aligned}$$\r:::\r\r### Example 8\r\r- Consider one red die and one white die.\r\r- Let the outcome be the ordered pair.\r\r- Assume these two dice is repeatedly cast under the same condition.\r\r- The cast of this pair of dice is a random experiment.\r\r- This is a random experiment.\r\r- Find the sample space ${\\mathcal{S}}$.\r\r### Example 8 (continued)\r\r- Assume the probability set function assigns a probability of 1/36 to each of the 36 points in ${\\mathcal{S}}$.\r\r- If $C_1 = \\{(1, 1), (2, 1), (3, 1), (4, 1), (5, 1)\\}$ and $C_2 = \\{(1, 2), (2, 2), (3,2), (4, 2), (5, 2)\\}$.\r\r- Find $P[C_1]$, $P[C_2]$, $P[C_1\\cup C_2]$ and $P[C_1\\cap C_2]$.\r\r### Inclusion exclusion formula\r\r- Consider a sequence of events $\\{C_n, n = 1, 2, \\}$.\r\r- Let $p_1 = P[C_1] + P[C_2] + P[C_3]$.\r\r- Let $p_2 = P[C_1 \\cap C_2] + P[C_1 \\cap C_3] + P[C_2 \\cap C_3]$.\r\r- Let $p_3 = P[C_1 \\cap C_2 \\cap C_3]$.\r\r- Then $$P[C_1 \\cup C_2 \\cup \\cdots \\cup C_k] = p_1 - p_2 + p_3 - \\cdots + (-1)^{n+1} p_n.$$\r\r### Bonferroni's inequality\r\r- Consider two events $C_1$ and $C_2$.\r\r- We have $$P[C_1\\cap C_2]\\ge P[C_1] + P[C_2] -1$$\r\r### Boole's inequality\r\r::: {#thm-ch1-7}\r- Let $\\{C_n\\}$ be an arbitrary sequence of events.\r\r- Then $$P\\left[ \\cup_{n=1}^{\\infty} C_n \\right]\\ \\le \\sum_{n=1}^{\\infty} P[C_n].$$\r:::\r\r### Equally likely principle\r\r- Let $x_i$, $i=1, \\cdots, m$, be all possible outcomes of an experiment.\r\r- Suppose $m$ is the total number of ways that the random experiment can terminate.\r\r- If each of $e_i$ has the same probability of occurring, $m$ outcomes are equally likely.\r\r- We have $$P[e_i]=\\frac{1}{m},~i=1,2,\\cdots, m.$$\r\r- Let $A\\in {\\mathcal{S}}$ and define $$P[A] = \\sum_{x_i\\in A} \\frac{1}{m} = \\frac{\\#(A)}{m}.$$\r\r### Example 9\r\r- Suppose $A$ includes $h$ possible number of outcomes.\r\r- Define $$P[A]=\\frac{h}{m}=\\frac{N(A)}{N(S)}$$ where\r\r  - $N(A)$ = $h$ = the number of ways $A$ can occur.\r\r  - $N(S)$ = $m$ = the number of ways $S$ can occur.\r\r### Example 10\r\r- Toss a fair coin twice\r\r- Possible outcomes: $TT, HT, TH, HH$\r\r- Assume $$P[TT]=P[HT]=P[TH]=P[HH]=\\frac{1}{4}$$\r\r- Consider $A$ = {at least one $H$} $$P[A] = P[HT + TH + HH] = P[HT] + P[TH] + P[HH] =\\frac{3}{4}$$\r\r### Counting rule -- Multiplication Principle\r\r- Suppose an experiment $E_1$ has $n_1$ outcomes.\r\r- Each of possible outcomes in $E_1$, an experiment has $n_2$ outcomes\r\r- The composite experiment $E_1E_2$ consisting of performing $E_1$ and then $E_2$ has $n_1n_2$ possible outcomes.\r\r### Example 11\r\r- Let $E_1$ denote the selection of a rat from a cage containing one female ($F$) and one male ($M$) rate.\r\r- Let $E_2$ denote the administering of either drug $A$, drug $B$ or a placebo $P$ to the selected rate.\r\r- The outcome of the composite experiment is $$(F, A), (F, B), (F,P), (M, A), (M, B), (M,P)$$\r\r::: center\r![](assets/graph/fig1-2-1.png)\r:::\r\r### More on multiplication Principle\r\r- Suppose experiment $E_i$ has $n_i$ outcomes, $i=1, 2, \\cdots, m$.\r\r- The composite experiment $E_1E_2\\cdots E_m$ has $n_1n_2\\cdots n_m$ possible outcomes.\r\r### Example 12 — Dinner choices\r\r- Choices of dinner\r\r  - $E_1$: soup or tomato juice\r\r  - $E_2$: steak or shrimp\r\r  - $E_3$: French fried potatoes, mashed potatoes or a baked potatoes\r\r  - $E_4$: corn or pea\r\r  - $E_5$: jelly, tossed salad, cottage cheese or coleslaw\r\r  - $E_6$: cake, cookies, pudding, brownie, vanilla ice cream, chocolate ice cream or orange sherbet\r\r  - $E_7$: coffee, tea, milk or punch\r\r- How many different dinner selections are possible?\r\r### Counting methods\r\r- Permutation\r\r- Combination\r\r- Sampling with replacement\r\r- Sampling without replacement\r\r- Ordered sample\r\r- Unordered sample\r\r### Common counting rules\r\r- Let $A$ be a set with $n$ elements.\r\r- A permutation of $n$ objects equals $n!$.\r\r- Suppose $k$-tuples whose components are elements of $A$, then there are $n^k$ such $k$-tuples whose components are elements of $A$.\r\r- Suppose $k\u003cn$ and we are interest in $k$-tuples whose elements are distinct. Then, the number of $k$ permutations taken from $A$ equals $$P_{k}^n = \\frac{n!}{(n-k)!}.$$\r\r### Common counting rules\r\r- Let $A$ be a set with $n$ elements.\r\r- Suppose $k\u003cn$ and we are interest in $k$-tuples whose elements are distinct.\r\r- Suppose the order is not important.\r\r- Then, the number of $k$ permutations taken from $A$ equals $$C_{k}^n = {n\\choose k} = \\frac{n!}{k!(n-k)!}.$$\r\r### Remark\r\r- $C_r^n = {n\\choose r}$ is called binomial coefficients.\r\r- The binomial expansion $$(a+b)^n =\\sum_{r=1}^n  {n\\choose r} b^ra^{n-r}$$\r\r### Definition\r\r::: {#def-ch1-2}\rEach of the $$C_k^n = {n\\choose k} =\\frac{n!}{k!(n-k)!}$$ permutations of $n$ objects, $k$ of one type and $n-r$ of another type, is called a distinguishable permutation.\r:::\r\r### Remark\r\r- Suppose there are $n$ objects.\r\r- $n_1$ are similar, $n_2$ are similar, $\\cdots$, $n_s$ are similar.\r\r- $n=\\sum_{i=1}^s n_i$, $0\\le n_i\\le n$.\r\r- The number of distinguishable permutations of the $n$ objects is $${n\\choose n_1, n_2, \\cdots, n_s} =\\frac{n!}{n_1!n_2!\\cdots n_s!}$$\r\r- It is called multinomial coefficients.\r\r### Monotone (nondecreasing) sets\r\r- Let $A_1,A_2,\\ldots, A_n, \\ldots$ be a sequence of nondecreasing sets, i.e., $$A_n \\subset A_{n+1}, n = 1, 2, 3, \\ldots$$\r\r- Define $$\\lim_{n\\rightarrow \\infty} A_n = \\cup_{n=1}^\\infty A_n.$$\r\r### Monotone (nonincreasing) sets\r\r- Let $A_1,A_2,\\ldots, A_n, \\ldots$ be a sequence of nonincreasing sets, i.e., $$A_n \\supset A_{n+1}, n = 1, 2, 3, \\ldots$$\r\r- Define $$\\lim_{n\\rightarrow \\infty} A_n = \\cap_{n=1}^\\infty A_n.$$\r\r### Properties of probability\r\r::: {#thm-ch1-8}\r- Let $\\{C_n\\}$ be a nondecreasing sequence of events.\r\r- Then $$\\lim_{n\\rightarrow\\infty} P[C_n] = P\\left[\\lim_{n\\rightarrow\\infty} C_n\\right] =\r  P\\left[ \\cup_{n=1}^{\\infty} C_n \\right].$$\r:::\r\r### Properties of probability\r\r::: {#thm-ch1-9}\r- Let $\\{C_n\\}$ be a nonincreasing sequence of events.\r\r- Then $$\\lim_{n\\rightarrow\\infty} P[C_n] = P\\left[\\lim_{n\\rightarrow\\infty} C_n\\right] =\r  P\\left[ \\cap_{n=1}^{\\infty} C_n \\right].$$\r:::\r\r## 1.4 Conditional Probability and Independence\r\r### Example 13\r\r::: center\r                 Early (E)   Late (L)   Totals\r  ------------ ----------- ---------- --------\r  Red (R)                5          8       13\r  Yellow (Y)             3          4        7\r  Totals                 8         12       20\r:::\r\r- $P[R|E]=\\frac{5}{8}=\\frac{N(R\\cap E)}{N(E)} = \\frac{N(R\\cap E)/20}{N(E)/20} = \\frac{P[R\\cap E]}{P[E]}$\r\r### Definition\r\r::: {#def-ch1-3}\rThe conditional probability of an event $A$, given that event $B$ has occurred, is defined by $$P[A|B]=\\frac{P[A\\cap B]}{P[B]}$$ provided that $P[B]>0$.\r:::\r\r### Example 14\r\r- A hand of 5 cards is to be dealt at random without replacement from an ordinary deck of 52 playing cards.\r\r- Let $C_1$ denote the event of at least 4 spades in the hand.\r\r- Let $C_2$ denote the event of an all-spade hand.\r\r- Find $P[C_2|C_1]$.\r\r### Properties\r\r- $P[A|B]\\ge 0$\r\r- $P[B|B]=1$\r\r- $P[A\\cap B] = P[B]\\times P[A|B]$ (Multiplication rule)\r\r### Properties\r\r- If $A_1, A_2, \\cdots$ are mutually exclusive events, then $$P[\\cup_{n=1}^{\\infty} A_n|B] = \\sum_{n=1}^\\infty P[A_n|B]$$\r\r### Example 15\r\r- A bowl contains eight chips.\r\r- Three of the chips are red and the remaining five are blue.\r\r- Two chips are to be drawn successively, at random without replacement.\r\r- Let $A$ denote the event of the first draw results in a red chip.\r\r- Let $B$ denote the event of the second draw results in a blue chip.\r\r- Find $P[A\\cap B]$.\r\r### Example 16\r\r- Consider an ordinary deck of playing cards.\r\r- Cards are to be drawn successively at random without replacement.\r\r- Compute the probability that the third spade appears in the first six cards drawn.\r\rDefine $$\\begin{aligned}\rA &= \\text{the event of two spades in the first five cards}\\\\\rB &= \\text{the event of a spade on the sixth draw}\\\\\r\\end{aligned}$$\r\r### Remark\r\r- The probability that three events $A$, $B$ and $C$, all occur is given by the multiplication rule, $$P[A\\cap B\\cap C] = P[A]P[B|A]P[C|A\\cap B]$$\r\r### Example 17\r\r- Four cards to be dealt successively at random and without replacement from an ordinary deck of playing cards.\r\r- What is the probability of receiving, in order, a spade, a heart, a diamond, and a club?\r\r### Law of total probability\r\r- Let $\\{C_i, i=1, 2, \\ldots, k\\}$ be a partition of ${\\mathcal{S}}$.\r\r- Let $C$ be an event.\r\r- Then $$P[C] = \\sum_{i = 1}^k P[C_i]\\times P[C|C_i]$$\r\r### Bayes' theorem\r\r- Let $\\{C_i, i=1, 2, \\ldots, k\\}$ be a partition of ${\\mathcal{S}}$.\r\r- Let $C$ be an event.\r\r- Suppose $P[C]>0$.\r\r- Then $$P[C_j|C] = \\frac{P[C_j]\\times P[C|C_j]}{\\sum_{i = 1}^k P[C_i]\\times P[C|C_i]}.$$\r\r### Example 18\r\r- The bowl $A_1$ contains three red and seven blue chips.\r\r- The bowl $A_2$ contains eight red and two blue chips.\r\r- All chips are identical in size and shape.\r\r- A die is cast and bowl $A_1$ is selected if five or six spots show on the side that is up; otherwise, bowl $A_2$ is selected.\r\r- Assign $P[A_1] = 2/6$ and $P[A_2] = 4/6$.\r\r- The selected bowl is handed to another person and one chip is taken at random.\r\r- Let $B$ denote an event of a red chip is taken.\r\r- Find $P[A_1|B]$ and $P[A_2|B]$.\r\r### Bayesian inference terminology\r\r- The prior probability -- the probability of $P[A_i]$.\r\r- The posterior probability -- the probability of $P[A_i|B]$.\r\r### Example 19\r\r- Three plants, $A_1$, $A_2$ and $A_3$, produce respectively, 10%, 50% and 40% of a company's output.\r\r- The percent of defective for $A_1$, $A_2$ and $A_3$ is 1%, 3% and 4%, respectively.\r\r- All products are sent to a central warehouse.\r\r- Let $B$ denote the event that one item is selected at random and observed to be defective.\r\r- Find $P[A_1|B]$, $P[A_2|B]$ and $P[A_3|B]$.\r\r### Example 20\r\r- Flip a fair coin twice and observe the sequence of heads and tails.\r\r- The sample space is $$S=\\{HH, HT, TH, TT\\}$$\r\r- Let $$\\begin{aligned}\r  A &= \\text{\\{heads on the first flip\\}}\\\\\r  B &=  \\text{\\{tails on the second flip\\}}\\\\\r  C &=  \\text{\\{tails on both flips\\}}\\\\\r  \\end{aligned}$$\r\r- Find $P[B|A]$\r\r### Definition\r\r::: {#def-ch1-4}\rEvents $A$ and $B$ are independent if and only if $$P[A\\cap B] = P[A]P[B]$$ Otherwise, $A$ and $B$ are called dependent events.\r:::\r\r### Remark\r\r- Independent events are also called statistically independent, stochastically independent or independent in a probabilistic sense.\r\r- The definition always holds If $P[A]=0$ or $P[B]=0$.\r\r### Example 21\r\r- A red die and a white die are rolled.\r\r- Let $A$ = {4 on the red die}.\r\r- Let $B$ = {sum of dice is odd}\r\r- Show that $A$ and $B$ are independent events.\r\r### Example 22\r\r- A red die and a white die are rolled.\r\r- Let $A$ = {5 one the red die}.\r\r- Let $B$ = {sum of dice is 11}\r\r- Show that $A$ and $B$ are dependent events.\r\r### Properties\r\r- If $A$ and $B$ are independent events, then the following pairs of events are also independent:\r\r  1.  $A$ and $B^{'}$\r\r  2.  $A^{'}$ and $B$\r\r  3.  $A^{'}$ and $B^{'}$\r\r### Example 23\r\r- An urn contains four balls numbered 1, 2, 3, and 4.\r\r- One ball is to be drawn at random from the urn.\r\r- Let $A=\\{1, 2\\}$, $B=\\{1,3\\}$ and $C=\\{1, 4\\}$.\r\r- Show that $A$ and $B$, $A$ and $C$, $B$ and $C$ are independent events, but not $A$, $B$ and $C$.\r\r### Definition\r\r::: {#def-ch1-5}\rEvents $A$, $B$ and $C$ are mutually independent if and only if the following two conditions hold:\r\r1.  $A$, $B$ and $C$ are pairwise independent; that is, $$P[A\\cap B]=P[A]P[B], P[A\\cap C]=P[A]P[C], P[B\\cap C]=P[B]P[C]$$\r\r2.  $P[A\\cap B\\cap C]=P[A]P[B]P[C]$\r:::\r\r### Properties\r\r- If $A$, $B$ and $C$ are mutually independent events, then the following pairs of events are also independent:\r\r  1.  $A$ and $B\\cap C$\r\r  2.  $A$ and $B\\cup C$\r\r  3.  $A^{'}$ and $B\\cap C^{'}$\r\r- $A^{'}$, $B^{'}$ and $C^{'}$ are mutually independent.\r\r### Remark\r\r- Many experiments consist of a sequence of $n$ trials that are mutually independent.\r\r- If the outcomes of the trials are not related to each other, then events, such that each is associated with a different trials, should be independent in the probability sense.\r\r- If $A_i$ is associated with the $i$th trials, $i=1, 2, \\ldots, n$, then $$P[A_1\\cap A_2\\cap \\cdots \\cap A_n]=P[A_1]P[A_2]\\cdots P[A_n]$$\r\r### Example 24\r\r- A compute system is built so that if component $K_1$ fails, it is bypassed and $K_2$ is used.\r\r- If $K_2$ fails, then $K_3$ is used.\r\r- Suppose that the probability that $K_1$ fails is 0.01, that $K_2$ fails is 0.03, and that $K_3$ fails is 0.08.\r\r- Assume that the failures are mutually independent events.\r\r- Find $P[\\text{Failure of the system}]$.\r\r## 1.5 Random Variables\r\r### Definition\r\r::: {#def-ch1-6}\r- Consider a random experiment with a sample space ${\\mathcal{S}}$.\r\r- A function $X$ assigns one and only one real number $X(c)=x$ to each element $c\\in {\\mathcal{S}}$.\r\r- $X$ is called a random variable.\r:::\r\r- The space or range of $X$ is the set of real numbers ${\\mathcal{D}} = \\{x: x = X(c), c\\in {\\mathcal{S}}\\}$.\r\r### Types of random variables\r\r- Discrete random variables -- ${\\mathcal{D}}$ is a countable set.\r\r- Continuous random variables -- ${\\mathcal{D}}$ is an interval of real numbers.\r\r### Distribution of a random variable\r\r- Given a random variable $X$, its range ${\\mathcal{D}}$ becomes the sample space of interest.\r\r- Besides inducing the sample space ${\\mathcal{D}}$, $X$ also induces a probability.\r\r- We call the distribution of $X$.\r\r### Discrete distribution\r\r- Let $X$ be a discrete random variable with a finite space ${\\mathcal{D}} = \\{d_1,\\ldots, d_m\\}$.\r\r- Define $$p_X(d_i) = P[\\{c: X(c) = d_i\\}], i = 1, 2, \\ldots, m.$$ which is called the probability mass function (pmf) of $X$.\r\r- The probability of event $D$ is $$P_X(D) = \\sum_{d_i\\in D} p_X(d_i), D\\subset {\\mathcal{D}}.$$\r\r### Example 25\r\r- Let $X$ be the sum of the upfaces on a roll of a pair of fair 6-sided dice, each with the numbers 1 through 6 on it.\r\r- The sample space is ${\\mathcal{S}} = \\{(i,j) : 1 ≤ i,j ≤ 6\\}$.\r\r- Find the pmf of $X$.\r\r### Example 25 (continued)\r\r- Let $B_1$ denote the event of 7 and 11.\r\r- Let $B_2$ denote the event of 2, 3 and 12.\r\r- Find $P_X[B_1]$ and $P_X[B_2]$.\r\r### Continuous distribution\r\r- Let $X$ be a continuous random variable with an interval of real numbers as the space ${\\mathcal{D}}$.\r\r- For the continuous random variables, the simple events of interest are intervals.\r\r- Determine a nonnegative function $f_X(x)$ such that for any interval of real numbers $(a,b) \\in {\\mathcal{D}}$, the induced probability distribution of $X$, $P_X(\\cdot)$, is defined as $$P_X[(a, b)] = P[\\{c\\in {\\mathcal{S}}: a \u003c X(c) \u003c b\\}] = \\int_a^b f_X(x) dx.$$ which is the probability that $X$ falls between $a$ and $b$ is the area under the curve $y = f_X(x)$ between $a$ and $b$.\r\r- The function $f_X$ is formally defined as the probability density function (pdf) of $X$.\r\r### Example 26\r\r- Let $X$ denote the outcome when a point is selected at random from an interval $[0,1]$.\r\r- If the experiment is performed in a fair manner.\r\r- Assume $$P_X[(a, b)] = b - a, 0 \u003c a \u003c b \u003c1.$$ which is the length of the interval.\r\r- The pdf of $X$ is $$f_X(x) = \\left\\{\\begin{array}{cc}\r  1 & 0\u003cx\u003c1\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\\right ..$$\r\r- Find $P\\left[\\left\\{X\u003c\\frac{1}{8}\\right\\} \\cup \\left\\{X>\\frac{7}{8}\\right\\}\\right]$.\r\r### Example 26 (continued)\r\r- For any point $a$, $0\u003ca\u003c1$.\r\r- Choose $n_0$ such that $0\u003ca-n_0^{-1}\u003ca\u003ca+n_0^{-1}\u003c1$.\r\r- We have $$P[X=a] \\le P\\left[a - \\frac{1}{n} \u003c X \u003c a+\\frac{1}{n}\\right] = \\frac{2}{n}, \\forall n\\ge n_0.$$\r\r- Since $2/n\\rightarrow 0$ as $n\\rightarrow \\infty$ and $a$ is arbitrary, we conclude that $P(X = a) = 0$ for all $a \\in (0, 1)$.\r\r### Cumulative distribution function\r\r- Let $X$ be a random variable.\r\r- Define $$\\begin{aligned}\r  F_X(x) &= P_X[ (-\\infty, x)] = P[X\\le x] \\\\\r  &= P[\\{c\\in {\\mathcal{S}}: X(c) \\le x\\}],~-\\infty\u003cx\u003c\\infty.\r  \\end{aligned}$$ the cumulative distribution function of $X$.\r\r- Use cdf as an abbreviate.\r\r### Example 27\r\r- Suppose a fair die with the numbers 1 through 6 on it is rolled.\r\r- Let $X$ be the upface of the roll.\r\r- Then the space of $X$ is 1,2,\\...,6 and its pmf is $$p_X(i)=1/6, i=1,2,...,6.$$\r\r- Find cdf of $X$.\r\r### Example 28\r\r- Let $X$ denote the outcome when a point is selected at random from an interval $[0,1]$.\r\r- If the experiment is performed in a fair manner.\r\r- Assume $$P_X[(a, b)] = b - a, 0 \u003c a \u003c b \u003c1.$$ which is the length of the interval.\r\r- The pdf of $X$ is $$f_X(x) = \\left\\{\\begin{array}{cc}\r  1 & 0\u003cx\u003c1\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\\right ..$$\r\r- Find cdf of $X$.\r\r### Example 28 (continued)\r\r- $F_X(x)$ and $f_X(x)$ are related, i.e., $$F_X(x) = \\int_{-\\infty}^{x} f_X(t) dt, ~\\forall x\\in R$$ and $$\\frac{d}{dx} F_X(x) = f_X(x), \\forall x\\in R,~\\text{except for }x=0\\text{ and }x=1.$$\r\r### Remark\r\r- $X$ and $Y$ are equal in distribution and write $X \\stackrel{D}{=} Y$ if and only if $F_X(x) = F_Y(x)$, for all $x \\in R$.\r\r### Example 29\r\r- Let $X$ denote the outcome when a point is selected at random from an interval $[0,1]$.\r\r- Define the random variable $Y$ as $Y = 1-X$.\r\r- $Y\\ne X$.\r\r- The space of $Y$ is the interval (0,1), the same as $X$.\r\r- The cdf of $Y$ is $$F_Y(y) = \\left\\{\\begin{array}{cc}\r  0 & y\u003c1\\\\\r  P[Y\\le y] =  1 - (1 - y) = y & 0\\le y\u003c1\\\\\r  1 & y\\ge 1\r  \\end{array}\r  \\right . .$$\r\r- $Y$ has the same cdf as $X$, i.e., $X \\stackrel{D}{=} Y$, but $Y\\ne X$.\r\r### Properties of cdf\r\r::: {#thm-ch1-10}\r- Let $X$ be a random variable with cumulative distribution function $F(x)$.\r\r- Then\r\r  - For all $a$ and $b$, if $a\u003cb$, then $F(a)\\le F(b)$ ($F$ is nondecreasing).\r\r  - $\\lim_{x\\rightarrow -\\infty} F(x) = 0$\r\r  - $\\lim_{x\\rightarrow \\infty} F(x) = 1$\r\r  - $\\lim_{x\\rightarrow x_0-} F(x) = F(x_0)$ ($F$ is right continuous).\r:::\r\r### Properties of cdf\r\r::: {#thm-ch1-11}\r- Let $X$ be a random variable with cumulative distribution function $F(x)$.\r\r- Then, for $a\u003cb$, $$P[a\u003c X \\le b] = F_X(b) - F_X(a).$$\r:::\r\r### Example 30\r\r- Let $X$ be the lifetime in years of a mechanical part.\r\r- Assume that $X$ has the cdf $$F_X(x) = \\left\\{\\begin{array}{cc}\r  0 & x\u003c0\\\\\r  1-e^{-x} & 0\\le x\r  \\end{array}\r  \\right ..$$\r\r- Find the pdf of $X$ and $P[1\u003cX\\le 3]$.\r\r### Properties of cdf\r\r::: {#thm-ch1-12}\r- Let $X$ be a random variable with cumulative distribution function $F(x)$.\r\r- Then, $$P[X=x] = F_X(x) - F_X(x-),$$ for all $x\\in R$, where $F_X(x-) = \\lim_{z\\rightarrow x-} F_X(z).$\r:::\r\r### Example 31\r\r- Let $X$ be the lifetime in years of a mechanical part.\r\r- Assume that $X$ has the cdf $$F_X(x) = \\left\\{\\begin{array}{cc}\r  0 & x\u003c0\\\\\r  1-e^{-x} & 0\\le x\r  \\end{array}\r  \\right ..$$\r\r- Find the pdf of $X$ and $P[a\u003cX\\le 3]$.\r\r### Properties of pdf or pmf\r\r- The total probability associated with a random variable $X$ of the discrete type with pmf $p_X (x)$ or of the continuous type with pdf $f_X (x)$ is 1.\r\r- It must be true that $$\\begin{array}{ccc}\r  \\sum_{x\\in {\\mathcal{D}}} p_X(x) = 1 & \\text{and} & \\int_{\\mathcal{D}} f_X(x)dx = 1,\r  \\end{array}$$ where ${\\mathcal{D}}$ is the space of X.\r\r### Example 32\r\r- Let $X$ has the pmf $$f_X(x) = \\left\\{\\begin{array}{cc}\r  cx & x = 1, 2, \\ldots, 10\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere},\r  \\end{array}\r  \\right .$$ for an appropriate constant $c$.\r\r- Find $c$.\r\r### Example 33\r\r- Let $X$ has the pdf $$f_X(x) = \\left\\{\\begin{array}{cc}\r  cx^3 & 0\u003cx\u003c2\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere},\r  \\end{array}\r  \\right .$$ for an appropriate constant $c$.\r\r- Find $c$.\r\r## Stage 2 — Deepen：從會算到會推導 {.unnumbered}\r\r本階段開始放慢。Sections 1.6--1.7 的前段定義是 Recall 與 Deepen 的橋接，課堂重點不是再次背誦 pmf / pdf，而是從\r\r$$\rX\\longrightarrow g(X)\\longrightarrow\\text{distribution of }g(X)\r$$\r\r進一步走向\r\r$$\rX_1,\\ldots,X_n\\longrightarrow T(X_1,\\ldots,X_n),\r$$\r\r為 sampling distribution 建立工具。接著以 Sections 1.8--1.9 深化 expectation、moments、variance 與 MGF。\r\r## 1.6 Discrete Random Variables\r\r### Discrete random variables\r\r::: {#def-ch1-7}\rA random variable is a discrete random variable if its space is either finite or countable.\r:::\r\r### Example 34\r\r- Toss a fair coin twice.\r\r- Let $X$ be the number of heads.\r\r### Example 35\r\r- Toss a four-sided die twice.\r\r- Let $X$ be the sum of the dots on the two tosses.\r\r### Probability mass function\r\r::: {#def-ch1-8}\r- Let $X$ be a discrete random variable with space ${\\mathcal{D}}$.\r\r- The probability mass function (pmf) of $X$ given by $$p_X(x) = P[X=x], \\text{for }x\\in {\\mathcal{D}}.$$\r:::\r\r### Properties of Probability mass function\r\r1.  $p_X(x)>0$, $x\\in {\\mathcal{D}}$.\r\r2.  $\\sum_{x\\in {\\mathcal{D}}} p_X(x)=1$.\r\r3.  $P[X\\in A]=\\sum_{x\\in A} p_X(x)$, where $A\\subset {\\mathcal{D}}$.\r\r### Support\r\r- Usually define $p_X(x)=0$ if $x\\not\\in S$.\r\r- The domain of $p_X(x)$ is the set of $R$.\r\r- To distinguish, the **support** of $X$ is the set of outcomes with positive probabilities.\r\r### Hypergeometric distribution\r\r- Consider a collection of $N=N_1+N_2$ similar objects.\r\r  - $N_1$ objects belong to the first class (red chip).\r\r  - $N_2$ objects belong to the second class (blue chip).\r\r- $n$ objects are randomly selected from $N$ objects.\r\r- Let $X$ be the number of objects drawing from the first class.\r\r- Find the pmf of $X$.\r\r### Example 36 — Hypergeometric distribution\r\r- There are 50 fishes in a small pond.\r\r- 10 of which have been tagged.\r\r- Suppose 7 fishes are caught randomly without replacement.\r\r- Find the probability that exactly 2 tagged fishes are caught.\r\r### Transformation\r\r- $X$ has a pmf $f(x)$.\r\r- Let $Y=u(X)$ for a given function $u(\\cdot)$.\r\r- Find the pmf of $Y$\r\r### Example 37\r\r- Consider a sequence of independent flips of a coin, each resulting in a head ($H$) or a tail ($T$).\r\r- Let $X$ be the flip number on which the first head appear.\r\r- Let $Y$ be the number of flips before the first head, i.e., $Y=X-1$.\r\r- Find the pmf of $Y$.\r\r### Example 38\r\r- Let $X$ have the pmf $$p_X(x) = \\left\\{\r  \\begin{array}{ll}\r  \\frac{3!}{x!(3-x)!}\\left(\\frac{2}{3}\\right)^x \\left(\\frac{1}{3}\\right)^{3-x}, x= 0, 1, 2, 3\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\\right ..$$\r\r- Let $Y=X^2$.\r\r- Find the pmf of $Y$.\r\r### Example 39\r\r- Consider a sequence of independent flops of a coin, each resulting in a head ($H$) or a tail ($T$).\r\r- Let $X$ be the flip number on which the first head appear.\r\r- If the first head appears on an odd number of flips, we pay the house one dollar, while if it appears on an even number of flips, we win one dollar from the house.\r\r- Let Y denote our net gain.\r\r- The space of $Y$ is -1, 1\r\r- Find the pmf of $Y$.\r\r### Example 40\r\r- Consider a sequence of independent flops of a coin, each resulting in a head ($H$) or a tail ($T$).\r\r- Let $X$ be the flip number on which the first head appear.\r\r- Let $Z = (X-2)^2$.\r\r- Find the pmf of $Z$.\r\r## 1.7 Continuous Random Variables\r\r### Continuous random variables\r\r::: {#def-ch1-9}\rA random variable is a continuous random variable if the cdf $F_X(x)$ is a continuous function for all $x\\in R$.\r:::\r\r### Probability density function\r\r- Most continuous random variables are absolutely continuous; that is, $$F_X(x) = \\int_{-\\infty}^x f_X(t) dt,$$ for some function $f_X (t)$.\r\r- The function $f_X (t)$ is called a probability density function (pdf) of X.\r\r- If $f_X (t)$ is also continuous, then the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus implies that $$\\frac{d}{dx} F_X(x) = f_X (x).$$\r\r### Support\r\r- The support of a continuous random variable X consists of all points $x$ such that $f_X(x) > 0$.\r\r- Denote the support of $X$ by $S$.\r\r### Properties of pdf\r\r- The pdf $f(x)$ of $X$ of the continuous type with space $S$.\r\r- $S$ is an interval or union of intervals.\r\r- $f(x)$ satisfies\r\r  - $f(x)\\ge 0$, $x\\in S$.\r\r  - $\\int_S f(x) dx=1$.\r\r  - If $(a, b)\\subseteq S$, then $$P[a\u003cX\u003cb]=\\int_a^b f(x)dx.$$\r\r### Uniform distribution\r\r- $X$ has a uniform distribution if $$f(x)  = \\frac{1}{b-a}, a\\le x\\le b$$ and 0 elsewhere.\r\r- Denote $U(a,b)$.\r\r### Example 41\r\r- Suppose a point is selected at random in the interior of a circle of radius 1.\r\r- Let $X$ be the distance of the selected point from the origin.\r\r- The sample space for the experiment is $${\\mathcal{S}} = \\{(w, y) : w^2 + y^2 \u003c 1\\}.$$\r\r- Assume subsets of ${\\mathcal{S}}$ which have equal area are equilikely.\r\r- Hence, the probability of the selected point lying in a set $A \\subset {\\mathcal{S}}$ is proportional to the area of A; i.e., $$P[A] = \\frac{\\text{area of A}}{\\pi}$$\r\r- Find cdf and pdf of $X$.\r\r### Example 42\r\r- Let the random variable be the time in seconds between incoming telephone calls at a busy switchboard.\r\r- Suppose that a reasonable probability model for $X$ is given by the pdf $$f_X(x) = \\left \\{\\begin{array}{ll}\r  \\frac{1}{4}e^{-x/4} & 0\u003cx\u003c\\infty\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\\right ..$$\r\r- Find cdf of $X$.\r\r- Find the probability that the time between successive phone calls exceeds 4 seconds.\r\r### Quantiles\r\r::: {#def-ch1-10}\r- Let $0 \u003c p \u003c 1$.\r\r- The quantile of order $p$ of the distribution of a random variable $X$ is a value $\\xi_p$ such that $P(X \u003c \\xi_p) \\le p$ and $P(X \\le \\xi_p) \\ge p$.\r\r- It is also known as the (100$p$)th percentile of $X$.\r:::\r\r### Quartiles\r\r::: {#def-ch1-11}\r- The quantile of order $0.25$, $0.5, 0.75$ of the distribution of a random variable $X$ is called the first quartile ($q_1$), median and the third quartile ($q_3$).\r\r- The difference $IQ = q_3 -q_1$ is called the interquartile range of $X$.\r:::\r\r### Note\r\r- Quantiles need not be unique even for continuous random variables with pdfs.\r\r- Any point in the interval (2,3) serves as a median for the following pdf: $$f_X(x) = \\left \\{\\begin{array}{ll}\r  3(1-x)(x-2) & 1\u003cx\u003c2\\\\\r  3(3-x)(x-4) & 3\u003cx\u003c4\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\\right ..$$\r\r### Note\r\r- A quantile, say $\\xi_p$, is in the support of an absolutely continuous random variable $X$ with cdf $F_X (x)$ then $\\xi_p$ is the unique solution to the equation: $$\\xi_p = F_X^{-1}(p),$$ where $F_X^{-1} (u)$ is the inverse function of $F_X (x)$.\r\r### Distribution function technique\r\r- Let $X$ be a random variable of the continuous type.\r\r- Let $Y=u(X)$, where $u(\\cdot)$ is a known function.\r\r- Then the pdf of $Y$ can be derived from $$G(y)=P[Y\\le y] = P[u(X)\\le y]$$\r\r### Example 43 — Log gamma distribution\r\r- Let $X$ have a gamma distribution with pdf $$f(x)=\\frac{1}{\\Gamma(\\alpha) \\theta^\\alpha} x^{\\alpha-1} e^{-x/\\theta}, 0\u003cx\u003c\\infty,$$ where $\\alpha>0$ and $\\theta>0$.\r\r- Let $Y=\\ln(X)$.\r\r- Find the pdf of $Y$.\r\r### Theorem\r\r::: {#thm-ch1-13}\r- Let $X$ be a random variable of the continuous type with pdf $f(x)$ with support $S_X$.\r\r- Let $Y=g(X)$, where $g(\\cdot)$ is a one-to-one differentiable function on $S_X$.\r\r- Assume the inverse function $g$ by $x= g^{-1}(y)$ and let $$J = \\frac{dx}{dy} = \\frac{dg^{-1}(y)}{dy} (\\text{Jacobian})$$\r\r- Then the pdf of $Y$ is given by $$f_Y(y) = f_X(g^{-1}(y))|\\frac{dx}{dy}|, \\text{for }y\\in S_Y,$$ where $S_Y$ is the support of $Y$ and $S_Y = \\{y=g(x): x\\in S_X\\}$.\r:::\r\r### Example 44\r\r- Let $X$ have a gamma distribution with pdf $$f(x)=\\frac{1}{\\Gamma(\\alpha) \\theta^\\alpha} x^{\\alpha-1} e^{-x/\\theta}, 0\u003cx\u003c\\infty,$$ where $\\alpha>0$ and $\\theta>0$.\r\r- Let $Y=\\ln(X)$.\r\r- Find the pdf of $Y$.\r\r### Example 45 — Log gamma distribution\r\r- Let $X$ have pdf $$f(x)=3(1-x)^2, 0\u003cx\u003c1.$$\r\r- Let $Y=(1-X)^3$.\r\r- Find the pdf of $Y$.\r\r### Example 46 — Mixture type of distribution\r\r- Let $X$ have cdf $$F(x) = \\left\\{\r  \\begin{array}{ll}\r  0 & x\u003c0\\\\\r  \\frac{x+1}{2} & 0\\le x\u003c1\\\\\r  1 & 1\\le x\r  \\end{array}\r  \\right ..$$\r\r- Find $P[-3\u003cX \\le \\frac{1}{2}]$ and $P[X=0]$.\r\r## 1.8 Expectation of a Random Variable\r\r### Expectation -- Continuous\r\r::: {#def-ch1-12}\r- Let $X$ be a random variable.\r\r- If $X$ with a continuous random variable with pdf $f(x)$ and $$\\int_{-\\infty}^{\\infty} |x| f(x) dx \u003c \\infty$$ then the expectation of $X$ is $$E[X] = \\int_{-\\infty}^{\\infty} x f(x) dx.$$\r:::\r\r### Expectation -- Discrete\r\r::: {#def-ch1-13}\r- Let $X$ be a random variable.\r\r- If $X$ with a pmf $p(x)$ and $$\\sum_{x\\in S} |x|p(x)$$ then the expectation of $X$ is $$E[X]=\\sum_{x\\in S} x p(x).$$\r:::\r\r### Note\r\r- The expectation E(X) is called the mathematical expectation of X, the expected value of X, or the mean of X.\r\r- When the mean designation is used, $E(X)$ is denoted by $\\mu$; i.e, $\\mu$ = $E(X)$.\r\r### Example 47\r\r- Let $X$ have a pmf $$f(x)=\\frac{1}{3}, x\\in S_X,$$ where $S_X = \\{-1, 0, 1\\}.$\r\r- Find $E[X]$.\r\r### Example 48\r\r- Let $X$ have a pdf $$f(x)=\\left\\{\r  \\begin{array}{ll}\r  4x^3 & 0\u003cx\u003c1\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\r  \\right ..$$\r\r- Find $E[X]$.\r\r### Expectation of a function of random variable (continuous)\r\r::: {#thm-ch1-14}\r- Let $X$ be a random variable and let $Y = g(X)$ for some function $g$.\r\r- If $X$ with a continuous random variable with pdf $f_X(x)$ and $$\\int_{-\\infty}^{\\infty} |g(x)| f_X(x) dx \u003c \\infty$$ then the expectation of $X$ is $$E[Y] = \\int_{-\\infty}^{\\infty} g(x) f(x) dx.$$\r:::\r\r### Expectation of a function of random variable (discrete)\r\r::: {#thm-ch1-15}\r- Let $X$ be a random variable and let $Y = g(X)$ for some function $g$.\r\r- If $X$ with a pmf $p(x)$ and $$\\sum_{x\\in S_X} |g(x)|p(x)$$ then the expectation of $X$ is $$E[Y]=\\sum_{x\\in S_X} g(x) p(x).$$\r:::\r\r### Example 49\r\r- Consider a sequence of independent flips of a coin, each resulting in a head (H) or a tail (T).\r\r- Let $X$ was the flip number on which the first head appeared.\r\r- Let $Y$ be the number of flips before the first head.\r\r- Let $Z = e^{-Y}$.\r\r- Find $E[Z]$.\r\r### Example 50\r\r- Let $X$ be a continuous random variable with the pdf $f(x) = 2x$ which has support on the interval (0, 1).\r\r- Let $Y=1/(1+X)$.\r\r- Find $E[Y]$.\r\r### Theorem\r\r::: {#thm-ch1-16}\rWhen the expectation exists, it satisfies the following properties:\r\r- If $c$ is a constant, then $E[c]=c$.\r\r- If $c$ is a constant and $u(\\cdot)$ is a function, then $$E[cu(X)]=cE[u(X)].$$\r\r- If $c_1$ and $c_2$ are constants and $u_1$ and $u_2$ are functions, then $$E[c_1u_1(X)+c_2u_2(X)]=c_1E[u_1(X)]+c_2E[u_2(X)].$$\r:::\r\r### Comment\r\r- If $c_1$, $\\cdots$ and $c_k$ are constants and $u_1$, $u_2$, $\\cdots$ and $u_k$ are functions, then $$E\\left[\\sum_{i=1}^kc_iu_i(X)\\right]=\\sum_{i=1}^kc_iE[u_i(X)].$$\r\r### Example 51\r\r- Let $X$ have the pdf $$f(x) = \\left\\{\\begin{array}{ll}\r  2(1-x) & 0\u003cx\u003c1\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\r  \\right ..$$\r\r- Find $E[X]$, $E[X^2]$ and $E[6X+3X^2]$.\r\r### Example 52\r\r- Let $X$ have the pmf $$f(x) = \\left\\{\\begin{array}{ll}\r  \\frac{x}{6} & x = 1, 2, 3\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\r  \\right ..$$\r\r- Find $E[6X^3+X]$.\r\r### Example 53\r\r- Let us divide, at random, a horizontal line segment of length 5 into two parts.\r\r- If $X$ is the length of the left-hand part, it is reasonable to assume $X$ has the pdf $$f(x) = \\left\\{\\begin{array}{ll}\r  \\frac{1}{5} & 0\u003cx\u003c5\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\r  \\right ..$$\r\r- Find $E[X(5-X)]$.\r\r## 1.9 Some Special Expectations\r\r### Settings\r\r- Let $X$ be a discrete random variable.\r\r- Let the support of $X$ be denoted as $S_X=\\{u_1, u_2, \\cdots, u_k\\}$.\r\r- Assume $$P[X=u_i]=f(u_i)=p_i, i=1, 2, \\cdots, k$$ and $$\\sum_{i=1}^k p_i=1.$$\r\r### Definition of mean\r\r::: {#def-ch1-14}\r- Let $X$ be a random variable whose expectation exists.\r\r- The mean value $\\mu$ of $X$ is defined to be $\\mu=E(X)$.\r:::\r\r### Definition of variance\r\r::: {#def-ch1-15}\r- Let $X$ be a random variable with finite mean $\\mu$ and such that $E[(X-\\mu)^2]\u003c\\infty$.\r\r- The variance of $X$ is defined to be $E[(X-\\mu)^2]$.\r\r- It is usually denoted by $\\sigma^2$ or by Var($X$).\r:::\r\r### Example 54\r\r- Consider a discrete random variable $X$ has a support $S_X=\\{1, 2, 3\\}$.\r\r- Let $f(1)=\\frac{3}{6}$, $f(2)=\\frac{2}{6}$, $f(3)=\\frac{1}{6}$.\r\r- Find $E[X]$ and $\\operatorname{Var}(X)$.\r\r### Comment\r\r- Show that $$\\operatorname{Var}(X)=E[X^2]-\\mu^2,$$\r\r- The standard deviation of $X$ is the positive square root of variance and is denoted as $\\sigma$.\r\r### Example 55\r\r- Let $a$ and $b$ be constants.\r\r- Let $Y=aX+b$.\r\r- Find $E[Y]$ and $\\operatorname{Var}(Y)$.\r\r### Example 56\r\r- Suppose the random variable $X$ has a uniform distribution with pdf $$f_X(x) = 1/(2a), -a \u003c x \u003c a,$$ zero elsewhere.\r\r- Find $\\mu$ and $\\sigma^2$.\r\r### Example 57\r\r- Let $X$ has the pdf $$f(x) = \\left\\{\\begin{array}{ll}\r  \\frac{1}{2}(x+1) & -1\u003cx\u003c1\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\r  \\right ..$$\r\r- Find $\\mu$ and $\\sigma^2$.\r\r### Example 58\r\r- Let $X$ has the pdf $$f(x) = \\left\\{\\begin{array}{ll}\r  \\frac{1}{x^2} & 1\u003cx\u003c\\infty\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\r  \\right ..$$\r\r- Find $\\mu$.\r\r### Moment\r\r- The $r$th moment is defined as $$E[X^r] = \\sum_{x\\in S} x^{r} f(x).$$\r\r- The $r$th moment about $b$ is defined as $$E[(X-b)^r] = \\sum_{x\\in S} (x-b)^{r} f(x).$$\r\r- The $r$th factorial moment is defined as $$E[(X)_r]=E[X(X-1)\\cdots (X-r+1)].$$\r\r### Example 59 — Hypergeometric distribution\r\r- Let $X$ have a hypergeometric distribution where $n$ objects are selected from $N=N_1+N_2$.\r\r- Find $\\operatorname{Var}(X)$.\r\r### Moment generating function\r\r::: {#def-ch1-16}\r- Let $X$ be a random variable.\r\r- If there is a $h>0$ such that $$E[e^{tX}]=\\sum_{x\\in S}e^{tx}f(x)$$ exists and is finite for $-h\u003ct\u003ch$, then the function $$M(t)=E[e^{tX}]$$ is called the moment-generating function (mgf) of $X$.\r:::\r\r### Comments\r\r- If $t=0$, $M(0)=1$.\r\r- If $S=\\{b_1, b_2, \\cdots\\}$, the mgf is $$M(t)=e^{tb_1}f(b_1) +e^{tb_2}f(b_2)+e^{tb_3}f(b_3)+\\cdots,$$\r\r- The coefficient of $e^{tb_i}$ is $$f(b_i)=P[X=b_i]$$\r\r### Comments\r\r- Consider two random variables had two pmfs $f(x)$ and $g(y)$.\r\r- Suppose the same space $S=\\{b_1, b_2, \\cdots, \\}$ is defined.\r\r- If $$\\begin{aligned}\r  e^{tb_1}f(b_1) &+e^{tb_2}f(b_2)+e^{tb_3}f(b_3)+\\cdots\\\\\r  &=e^{tb_1}g(b_1) +e^{tb_2}g(b_2)+e^{tb_3}g(b_3)+\\cdots,\r  \\end{aligned}$$ for all $t$, $-h\u003ct\u003ch$, then $$f(b_i)=g(b_i), \\forall i$$\r\r### Example 60\r\r- Suppose we have a fair spinner with the numbers 1, 2, and 3 on it.\r\r- Let $X$ be the number of spins until the first 3 occurs.\r\r- Assuming that the spins are independent, the pmf of $X$ is $$p(x) = \\frac{1}{3}\\left(\\frac{2}{3}\\right)^{x-1}, x= 1, 2, 3, \\ldots.$$\r\r- Find the mgf of $X$.\r\r### Equivalence\r\r::: {#thm-ch1-17}\r- Let $X$ and $Y$ be random variables with moment generating functions $M_X(\\cdot)$ and $M_Y(\\cdot)$, respectively, existing in open intervals about 0.\r\r- Then $F_X (z) = F_Y (z)$ for all $z\\in R$ if and only if $M_X(t) = M_Y (t)$ for all $t \\in (-h,h)$ for some $h > 0$.\r:::\r\r### Example 61\r\r- Suppose $X$ has the mgf $$M(t)=e^t\\left(\\frac{1}{10}\\right)+e^{2t}\\left(\\frac{2}{10}\\right)+e^{3t}\\left(\\frac{3}{10}\\right)+e^{4t}\\left(\\frac{4}{10}\\right), ~-\\infty\u003ct\u003c\\infty$$\r\r- Find the pmf of $X$.\r\r### Example 62\r\r- Show that $M^{'}(0)=\\mu$ and $M^{''}(0)-[M^{'}(0)]^2=\\sigma^2$.\r\r### Note\r\r- If $m$ is a positive integer and $M^{(m)}(t)$ denotes the $m$th derivative of $M(t)$.\r\r- Then $M^{(m)}(0)=E[X^m]$.\r\r## Stage 3 — Connect：由機率工具走向數理統計 {.unnumbered}\r\r本階段不把 inequalities 當作孤立公式。Markov、Chebyshev 與 Jensen 分別提供尾端機率控制、集中現象及凸性比較；其中 Chebyshev 不等式會直接連接樣本平均數與依機率收斂，為後續抽樣分配、漸近理論與統計推論鋪路。\r\r## 1.10 Important Inequalities\r\r### Existence of moments\r\r- Let $X$ be a random variable and let $m$ be a positive integer.\r\r- Suppose $E[X^m]$ exists.\r\r- If $k$ is a positive integer and $k \\le m$, then $E[X^k]$ exists.\r\r### Markov's Inequality\r\r::: {#thm-ch1-18}\r- Let $u(X)$ be a nonnegative function of the random variable $X$.\r\r- If $E[u(X)]\u003c\\infty$. random variable and let m be a positive integer.\r\r- For every positive constant $c$, $$P[u(X)\\ge c]\\le \\frac{E[u(X)]}{c}.$$\r:::\r\r### Chebyshev's Inequality\r\r::: {#thm-ch1-19}\r- Let $X$ be a random variable with finite variance $\\sigma^2$.\r\r- For every positive constant $k$, $$P[|X-\\mu|\\ge k\\sigma]\\le \\frac{1}{k^2}.$$\r:::\r\r### Example 63\r\r- Let $X$ has the pdf $$f(x) = \\left\\{\\begin{array}{ll}\r  \\frac{1}{2\\sqrt{3}} & -\\sqrt{3}\u003cx\u003c\\sqrt{3}\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\r  \\right ..$$\r\r- Find $P[|X-\\mu|\\ge k\\sigma]$ and the upper bound.\r\r### Example 64\r\r- Let the random variable $X$ of the discrete type have probabilities 1/8, 6/8, 1/8 at the points $x=-1,0,1$, respectively.\r\r- Find $P[|X-\\mu|\\ge k\\sigma]$ and the upper bound.\r\r### Connect: sample mean and Chebyshev\r\r令 $X_1,\\ldots,X_n$ 為獨立同分配（iid）的隨機變數，且\r\r$$\rE(X_i)=\\mu,\\qquad \\operatorname{Var}(X_i)=\\sigma^2\u003c\\infty.\r$$\r\r樣本平均數為\r\r$$\r\\overline X_n=\\frac{1}{n}\\sum_{i=1}^n X_i.\r$$\r\r由期望值與獨立性，\r\r$$\rE(\\overline X_n)=\\mu,\r\\qquad\r\\operatorname{Var}(\\overline X_n)\r=\\frac{1}{n^2}\\sum_{i=1}^n\\operatorname{Var}(X_i)\r=\\frac{\\sigma^2}{n}.\r$$\r\r因此對任意 $\\varepsilon>0$，Chebyshev 不等式給出\r\r$$\rP\\!\\left(\\left|\\overline X_n-\\mu\\right|\\ge\\varepsilon\\right)\r\\le\r\\frac{\\operatorname{Var}(\\overline X_n)}{\\varepsilon^2}\r=\\frac{\\sigma^2}{n\\varepsilon^2}\r\\longrightarrow 0.\r$$\r\r這正是\r\r$$\r\\overline X_n\\xrightarrow{P}\\mu,\r$$\r\r也就是樣本平均數**依機率收斂**到母體平均數；這個結果是弱大數法則的一個版本。\r\r::: {.callout-tip title=\"往後連接\"}\r這裡的邏輯鏈是\r\r$$\r\\operatorname{Var}(\\overline X_n)=\\frac{\\sigma^2}{n}\r\\Longrightarrow\r\\text{Chebyshev bound}\\to0\r\\Longrightarrow\r\\overline X_n\\xrightarrow{P}\\mu.\r$$\r\r後續將把 $T(X_1,\\ldots,X_n)$ 視為 statistic，研究其 sampling distribution，並在漸近理論中系統整理 convergence in probability。參見 [Sampling and Inference](04-sampling-inference.qmd) 與 [Asymptotics](05-asymptotics.qmd)。\r:::\r\r### Convex function\r\r::: {#def-ch1-17}\r- A function $\\phi$ defined on an interval $(a, b)$, $-\\infty\u003c a \u003c b \u003c\\infty$, is said to be a convex function if for all $x, y$ in $(a, b)$ and for all $0 \u003c \\gamma \u003c 1$, $$\\phi(\\gamma x + (1 - \\gamma)y) \\le \\gamma \\phi(x) + (1 - \\gamma) \\phi(y).$$ We say $\\phi$ is strictly convex if the above inequality is strict.\r:::\r\r### Properties -- Convex function\r\r::: {#thm-ch1-20}\r- If $\\phi$ is differentiable on $(a, b)$, then\r\r  - $\\phi$ is convex if and only if $\\phi^{'}(x)\\le \\phi^{'}(y)$, for all $a\u003cx\u003cy\u003cb$.\r\r  - $\\phi$ is strictly convex if and only if $\\phi^{'}(x) \u003c \\phi^{'}(y)$, for all $a\u003cx\u003cy\u003cb$.\r\r- If $\\phi$ is twice differentiable on $(a, b)$, then\r\r  - $\\phi$ is convex if and only if $\\phi^{''}(x)\\ge 0$, for all $a\u003cx\u003cb$.\r\r  - $\\phi$ is strictly convex if and only if $\\phi^{''}(x) >0$, for all $a\u003cx\u003cb$.\r:::\r\r### Jensen's Inequality\r\r::: {#thm-ch1-21}\r- If $\\phi$ is convex on an open interval $I$ and $X$ is a random variable whose support is contained in $I$ and has finite expectation, then $$\\phi(E[X]) \\le E[\\phi(X)]$$\r\r- If $\\phi$ is strictly convex, then the inequality is strict unless $X$ is a constant random variable.\r:::\r\r### Example 65\r\r- Let $X$ be a nondegenerate random variable with mean $\\mu$ and a finite second moment.\r\r- Show that $(E[X])^2\\le E[X^2]$.\r\r### Example 66\r\r- Let $\\{a_1, . . . , a_n\\}$ be a set of positive numbers.\r\r- Create a distribution for a random variable $X$ by placing weight $1/n$ on each of the numbers $a_1, . . . , a_n$.\r\r- The arithmetic mean (AM) equals $$E[X] = \\frac{1}{n}\\sum_{i=1}^n a_i.$$\r\r- The geometric mean (GM) equals $(a_1a_2\\cdots a_n)^{1/n}$.\r\r- The harmonic mean (HM) equals $$\\frac{1}{\\frac{1}{n}\\sum_{i=1}^n \\frac{1}{a_i}}$$\r\r- Show that $HM\\le GM\\le AM$.\r\r## 本章摘要\r\r本章依 **Recall $\\to$ Deepen $\\to$ Connect** 建立後續數理統計所需的共同語言。Stage 1 快速喚醒機率模型、條件機率、隨機變數與分配；Stage 2 深化 transformation、$E[g(X)]$、moments、variance 與 MGF；Stage 3 以重要不等式連接樣本平均數、依機率收斂及後續推論理論。\r\r## 常見錯誤\r\r- 把事件與隨機變數混為一談\r- 使用密度值代替區間機率\r- 套用條件機率時忘記分母事件\r- 引用不等式時沒有檢查非負性或凸性條件\r\r## 章末練習\r\r1. 從本章選出三個關鍵定義，分別寫出數學形式、成立條件及一個例子。\r2. 選擇一個主要定理或方法，重做推導並標記每一步使用的假設。\r3. 從原講義例題挑選一題，完整寫出解題目標、方法選擇、計算與結果解釋。\r4. 設計一個會違反本章方法條件的反例，說明錯誤會出現在哪一步。\r\r::: {.callout-note title=\"授課提醒\"}\r本章保留完整材料供學生查閱，但「保留」不等於「逐頁等速講授」。Stage 1 可按班級診斷結果選題，Stage 2 應保留推導時間，Stage 3 則務必讓學生說明 Chebyshev 如何導出 $\\overline X_n\\xrightarrow{P}\\mu$。\r:::\r"},"elapsedMs":4}</script></tool-result></div>

# Structure of `01-probability.qmd`

This is a Quarto lecture document titled "機率與單變量分配" (Probability and Univariate Distributions), organized around a three-stage pedagogical arc — **Recall → Deepen → Connect** — with section numbering matching Hogg, McKean & Craig Ch. 1.

**Front matter**: Title, callout box explaining the chapter's teaching philosophy (review vs. re-teach), learning objectives, and prerequisite knowledge notes with pacing guidance for each stage.

**Stage 1 — Recall** (fast review, ~60–90 min):
- 1.1 Introduction (experiments, sample space, events, relative frequency)
- 1.2 Sets (algebra of sets, Venn diagrams, laws, countable unions/intersections, set functions, Examples 1–7)
- 1.3 Probability Set Function (axioms, theorems on complement/monotonicity/inclusion-exclusion/Boole's inequality, equally-likely principle, counting rules — permutations/combinations, Examples 8–12)
- 1.4 Conditional Probability and Independence (definition, multiplication rule, law of total probability, Bayes' theorem, independence/mutual independence, Examples 13–24)
- 1.5 Random Variables (definitions, discrete/continuous types, pmf/pdf, cdf and its properties, Examples 25–33)

**Stage 2 — Deepen** (transformations, expectation, moments):
- 1.6 Discrete Random Variables (pmf properties, support, hypergeometric distribution, transformation technique, Examples 34–40)
- 1.7 Continuous Random Variables (pdf properties, uniform distribution, quantiles/quartiles, distribution-function and Jacobian/transformation techniques, Examples 41–46)
- 1.8 Expectation of a Random Variable (definitions for discrete/continuous, expectation of $g(X)$, linearity properties, Examples 47–53)
- 1.9 Some Special Expectations (mean, variance, moments, factorial moments, moment generating functions and uniqueness theorem, Examples 54–62)

**Stage 3 — Connect** (bridging to inference/asymptotics):
- 1.10 Important Inequalities (Markov's, Chebyshev's inequalities, Examples 63–64; a dedicated "Connect" subsection deriving convergence in probability of the sample mean via Chebyshev, linking forward to sampling/asymptotics chapters; convexity, Jensen's inequality, AM-GM-HM example, Examples 65–66)

**Back matter**: Chapter summary, common mistakes list, end-of-chapter exercises, and a closing callout on pacing reminders.

The document numbers roughly 66 worked examples and ~21 formal definitions/theorems (tagged with `{#def-ch1-*}` / `{#thm-ch1-*}` cross-reference labels), interspersed with `###` subsection headers per topic/example.

The HTZ course (大二) already covers transformations through the distribution function technique, which duplicates what HMC covers in sections 1.6.1 and 1.7.1. This confirms that the probability document's transformation sections are largely review material.

The actual course plan appears to be teaching HMC Chapter 1 to junior-year students who already completed HTZ's introductory course as sophomores, which covered roughly equivalent foundational material in probability, sets, conditional probability, Bayes' theorem, and transformations of random variables.

The key difference is that HTZ approached these topics at a less rigorous level without the measure-theoretic formalism, and didn't formally cover things like MGF uniqueness, Jensen's inequality, convex functions, or the convergence-in-probability connection for sample means. Given that the qmd file's own structure (Recall→Deepen→Connect) already reflects the instructor's pedagogical intent, I should now compare the document against HMC 7e to assess completeness and fidelity, while also identifying what could be trimmed since it overlaps with HTZ. The genuinely novel content that should anchor the teaching focus includes the measure-theoretic foundations (monotone event sequences, probability continuity), the MGF uniqueness theorem with its formal existence-interval treatment, and Jensen's inequality—areas where HTZ provides computational coverage but lacks the theoretical rigor needed here.

Most importantly, section 1.10 on inequalities (Markov, Chebyshev, Jensen) deserves full treatment, especially the explicit connection between these inequalities and convergence in probability leading to the weak law of large numbers—this is the most exam and thesis-relevant material that genuinely extends beyond a standard intro course.

For the remaining sections, I should keep 1.3's formal axioms and continuity theorems plus 1.9's MGF uniqueness theorem since those provide the mathematical rigor students haven't encountered before. But I can move quickly through 1.1 and 1.4 on conditional probability and Bayes' rule—those are almost certainly familiar from HTZ, so just a diagnostic example or two rather than repeating all the foundational theorems. Similarly, 1.5 through 1.7 on pmf, pdf, and cdf definitions are review material, though the Jacobian transformation technique for change of variables is worth keeping since it's less commonly seen.

I also notice there's a verbatim duplicate of the monotone sets section appearing twice in the document, which looks like a transcription error that needs to be flagged and removed. I'm recalling the standard chapter structure for that textbook—Chapters 1 through 3 cover probability fundamentals, discrete distributions, and continuous distributions respectively, while Chapter 4 moves into bivariate distributions and correlation. The transformations section (Jacobian and MGF methods) overlaps completely with what's in qmd 1.6/1.7, and topics like the normal sample mean and CLT in HTZ 5.5-5.6 already venture into asymptotics that qmd will cover more rigorously in chapter 5. So essentially all of qmd's foundational material (chapters 1-9) duplicates what the students learned in HTZ chapters 1, 2, 3, and 5. The genuinely new content for third-year mathematical statistics is the rigorous axiomatic treatment with countable additivity and monotone class limit theorems, which HTZ doesn't cover. 先說結論：核對 `Textbook - 7e (2012).pdf` 的目錄後，[01-probability.qmd](01-probability.qmd) 的 1.1–1.10 節架構**與 HMC 7e 第 1 章「Probability and Distributions」逐節對應**，內容轉錄忠實。而 HTZ（大二用書）資料夾裡雖然沒有完整課本 PDF，但依據分節練習檔案（`0101`–`0507`）可還原其目錄，對照後可以判斷哪些內容在大二已經教過。

**HTZ（大二）已涵蓋範圍**（依標準目錄 + 檔案內容核對）：

| HTZ 章節 | 主題 | 對應 qmd 小節 |
|---|---|---|
| Ch1 (1.1–1.5) | 機率公理、計數法、條件機率、獨立性、Bayes | 1.1, 1.3, 1.4（部分 1.2） |
| Ch2 (2.1–2.6) | 離散型 r.v.、pmf、期望值、特殊期望、二項/負二項/Poisson | 1.5, 1.6, 1.8, 1.9（部分） |
| Ch3 (3.1–3.4) | 連續型 r.v.、pdf、指數/Gamma/常態、hazard function、期望 | 1.5, 1.7, 1.8 |
| Ch5 (5.1–5.7) | 一元/多元變數轉換（distribution function 法、Jacobian）、MGF 技巧、CLT 前置 | 1.6.1, 1.7.1（transformation）|

我實際讀了 `0304.tex`（Weibull、hazard function、$E[g(X)]$）與 `0501.tex`（distribution function technique + Jacobian，即 `thm-ch1-13` 的內容）確認：連 transformation 這塊，大二也已經用過同樣方法練習過。

**大二完全沒教過、真正屬於「大三數理統計」新內容的部分**：
- 1.2 中 monotone sequence of sets 的 $\lim_n A_n$ 定義、1.3 中 `thm-ch1-8`／`thm-ch1-9`（連續性定理）——這種公理化、極限式的處理在 HTZ 通常略過
- 1.9 中 MGF 唯一性定理（`thm-ch1-17`）的正式敘述與存在區間 $(-h,h)$ 的處理
- 1.10 整節：convex function 定義、Jensen's inequality、以及用 Chebyshev 推導 $\overline X_n \xrightarrow{P} \mu$ ——HTZ 目錄裡完全沒有 convexity/Jensen，Chebyshev 頂多在 CLT 章節（5.6）順帶一提，沒有這種形式化連結

---

### 具體建議

| 小節 | 建議 | 理由 |
|---|---|---|
| 1.1 Introduction | **縮短**，1 個例子即可 | 大二 Ch1 完全重複 |
| 1.2 Sets | **縮短**：De Morgan/分配律快速過；monotone sets 的極限定義**保留** | 集合代數重複，但極限式定義是新概念 |
| 1.3 Probability Set Function | **縮短**公理與 `thm-ch1-1`~`thm-ch1-7`；**保留** `thm-ch1-8`／`thm-ch1-9`（連續性） | 前者是大二內容，後者是新的極限工具 |
| 1.4 Conditional Prob. & Independence | **大幅縮短**，用 1–2 題診斷即可，不必重跑全部 Example 13–24 | 與大二 Ch1 幾乎逐題重複 |
| 1.5 Random Variables | **縮短**，pmf/pdf/cdf 定義快速帶過 | 大二 Ch2/Ch3 已教 |
| 1.6/1.7 Discrete/Continuous RV + Transformation | **縮短為快速回顧**，transformation 用 1 個 Jacobian 例子帶過即可 | HTZ 5.1 已用相同方法練習過，不必重複 Example 34–46 全數 |
| 1.8 Expectation | **縮短**，$E[g(X)]$ 性質快速確認 | 大二已練過 |
| 1.9 Special Expectations | **部分保留**：mean/variance 快速過；MGF 唯一性定理與存在區間**保留並深化** | 唯一性定理是新的理論工具 |
| 1.10 Important Inequalities | **完整保留、甚至可加重比例** | convexity、Jensen、Chebyshev→收斂於機率的推導完全是新內容，也是通往後續漸近理論章節的橋樑 |

另外提醒一個與課程規劃無關、但值得處理的問題：qmd 內「Monotone (nondecreasing/nonincreasing) sets」這兩小節**逐字重複出現兩次**（第 204–215 行與第 587–597 行），建議之後刪除重複段落。

整體來看，這份講義目前 Stage 1（1.1–1.5）與 Stage 2 前段（1.6–1.7）的篇幅遠大於實際教學價值——這些對大三生幾乎是二次重複；真正該花時間的是 1.9 的 MGF 唯一性定理與整個 1.10 節。您原先寫的「Recall → Deepen → Connect」定位是對的方向，只是目前 Stage 1 分量偏重，可以再壓縮。


<span class="suggestion">依上表縮短 1.1–1.7 節的例題數量，草擬修訂版 01-probability.qmd</span>


<span class="suggestion">刪除 qmd 中重複出現兩次的 monotone sets 段落</span>
