\"多變量分配與變數轉換（精簡草稿）\"\r---\r\r::: {.callout-tip title=\"本章定位\"}\r本章保留原有英文術語與公式，並整理為可連續閱讀的教學形式。\r:::\r\r::: {.callout-note title=\"本檔為修訂草稿\"}\r比對 Hogg, Tanis & Zimmerman（大二用書）Ch.4（雙變量分配）與 Ch.5.2（雙變量轉換）後發現：「Distributions of two random variables」「Transformations: Bivariate random variables」「Conditional Distributions and Expectations」「Independent random variables」「The correlation coefficient」五節與大二內容重疊度高，已精簡例題（原 38 題減至 23 題）。「Extension to several random variables」（$n$ 維隨機向量、covariance matrix、random matrix 代數）與一般 many-to-one 轉換在大二找不到對應內容，僅刪除少量重複技巧的例題，其餘完整保留。另外修正原稿中一個未編號的例題標題（`### Exampe` → `### Example 16 (continued)`）。\r:::\r\r## 學習目標\r\r完成本章後，學生應能：\r\r- 由聯合分配求邊際與條件分配\r- 處理二維與多維隨機變數轉換\r- 計算共變異數、相關係數與共變異矩陣\r- 判斷隨機變數的獨立性\r- 推導隨機變數線性組合的分配\r\r## 必備的先備知識\r\r第 1 章的分配函數、期望與單變量變數轉換。\r\r以下依原講義順序整理核心定義、定理、公式與例題。\r\r## Distributions of two random variables\r\r### Definition\r\r::: {#def-ch2-1}\r- Consider a random experiment with a sample space ${\\mathcal{C}}$.\r\r- Define consider two random variables $X_1$ and $X_2$.\r\r- Assign to each element $c$ of ${\\mathcal{C}}$ one and only one ordered pair of numbers $X_1(c) = x_1$, $X_2(c) = x_2$.\r\r- Then,\r\r  - $(X_1,X_2)$ is a random vector.\r\r  - The space of $(X_1,X_2)$ is the set of ordered pairs $${\\mathcal{D}} = \\{(x_1, x_2) : x_1 = X_1(c), x_2 = X_2(c), c \\in {\\mathcal{C}}\\}.$$\r:::\r\r### Cumulative distribution function (cdf)\r\r- Let $A\\subset {\\mathcal{D}}$.\r\r- Denote the probability of the event $A$ as $P_{X_1, X_2}[A]$.\r\r- $P_{X_1, X_2}[A]$ is uniquely determined by the cumulative distribution function (cdf), which is given by $$F_{X_1,X_2}(x_1,x_2)=P[\\{X_1 \\le x_1\\} \\cap \\{X_2 \\le x_2\\}],$$ for all $(x_1, x_2)\\in R^2$.\r\r### Properties of cdf\r\r- For all $a_1\u003cb_1$ and $a_2\u003cb_2$, show that $$\\begin{aligned}\r  \\lefteqn{P[a_1 \u003c X_1 \\le b_1,a_2 \u003cX_2 \\le b_2] }\\\\\r  & = F_{X_1,X_2}(b_1,b_2) -\r  F_{X_1,X_2}(a_1,b_2) - F_{X_1,X_2}(b_1,a_2) + F_{X_1,X_2}(a_1,a_2)\r  \\end{aligned}$$\r\r### Discrete random vector\r\r::: {#def-ch2-2}\r- Suppose ${\\mathcal{D}}$ is finite or countable.\r\r- Let $S$ denote the two-dimensional space of $X_1$ and $X_2$.\r\r- The joint probability mass function of $X_1$ and $X_2$. $$p_{X_1, X_2}(x_1,x_2)=P[X_1=x_1, X_2=x_2]$$ for all $(x_1, x_2)\\in {\\mathcal{D}}$.\r:::\r\r### Conditions for joint pmf\r\r- $0\\le p_{X_1, X_2}(x_1,x_2)\\le 1$.\r\r- $\\sum\\sum_{(x_1, x_2)\\in {\\mathcal{D}}} p_{X_1, X_2}(x_1,x_2)=1$.\r\r- $P[(X,Y)\\in A]=\\sum\\sum_{(x_1,x_2)\\in A} p_{X_1, X_2}(x_1,x_2)$.\r\r### Example 1 （查無 HMC 7e 直接對應例題，屬補充教材）\r\r- Roll a pair of fair dice.\r\r- Let $X$ denote the smaller and $Y$ the larger outcome on the dice.\r\r- Find the joint pmf.\r\r### Note\r\r- The support of a discrete random vector $(X_1, X_2)$ is defined as the space that $$\\{(x_1, x_2)| p_{X_1, X_2}(x_1,x_2)>0 \\}.$$\r\r- The definition of a pmf is extended to a convenient set by using zero elsewhere.\r\r### Continuous random vector\r\r::: {#def-ch2-3}\r- A random vector $(X_1, X_2)$ with space ${\\mathcal{D}}$ is of the continuous type if its cdf $F_{X_1,X_2}(x_1,x_2)$ is continuous.\r\r- $F_{X_1,X_2}(x_1,x_2)$ can be expressed as $$F_{X_1,X_2}(x_1,x_2) = \\int_{-\\infty}^{x_1}\\int_{-\\infty}^{x_2}\r  f_{X_1,X_2}(w_1,w_2)dw_1dw_2,$$ for all $(x_1, x_2)\\in {\\mathcal{D}}$.\r\r- The integrand is called the joint probability density function (pdf) of $(X_1, X_2)$.\r:::\r\r### Note\r\r- We have $$\\frac{\\partial^2 F_{X_1,X_2}(x_1,x_2)}{\\partial x_1\\partial x_2} = f_{X_1,X_2}(x_1,x_2)$$ except possibly on events that have probability zero.\r\r### Conditions for joint pdf\r\r- $0\\le f_{X_1, X_2}(x_1,x_2)$.\r\r- $\\int\\int_{(x_1, x_2)\\in {\\mathcal{D}}} f_{X_1, X_2}(x_1,x_2)dx_1dx_2=1$.\r\r- For an event $A$, $$P[(X,Y)\\in A]=\\int\\int_{(x_1,x_2)\\in A} f_{X_1, X_2}(x_1,x_2)dx_1dx_2.$$\r\r### Note\r\r- The support of a continuous random vector $(X_1, X_2)$ is defined as the space that $$\\{(x_1, x_2)| f_{X_1, X_2}(x_1,x_2)>0 \\}.$$\r\r- The support is denoted as ${\\mathcal{S}}$.\r\r- The definition of a pdf is extended to $R^2$ by using zero elsewhere.\r\r### Special event\r\r- Let ($X_1$, $X_2$) be a random vector.\r\r- Consider the event $\\{X_1\\le x_1\\}$.\r\r- We have $$\\{X_1\\le x_1\\} = \\{X_1\\le x_1\\} \\cap \\{-\\infty\u003cX_2\u003c\\infty\\}$$\r\r- The probability of this event is $$F_{X_1}(x_1) = P[X_1\\le x_1, -\\infty\u003cX_2\u003c\\infty]$$ for all $x_1\\in R$.\r\r### Marginal pmf\r\r- Let ($X_1$, $X_2$) be a discrete random vector.\r\r- Find the probability of $\\{X_1\\le x_1\\}$ and the pmf of $X_1$.\r\r### Example 5 （查無 HMC 7e 直接對應例題，屬補充教材）\r\r- Let the joint pmf of $X$ and $Y$ be defined by $$f(x,y)=\\frac{x+y}{21}, x=1, 2, 3; y=1, 2.$$\r\r- Find $f_X(x)$ and $f_Y(y)$.\r\r### Marginal pdf\r\r- Let ($X_1$, $X_2$) be a continuous random vector.\r\r- Find the probability of $\\{X_1\\le x_1\\}$ and the pdf of $X_1$.\r\r### Example 7 （對應課本 Example 2.1.4）\r\r- Let $X$ and $Y$ have the joint pdf$$f(x,y) = \\left\\{\\begin{array}{ll}\r  x+y & 0\u003cx\u003c1, 0\u003cy\u003c1\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\\right..$$\r\r- Find the marginal pdf of $X$ and $Y$, respectively.\r\r- Find $P[X+Y\\le 1]$.\r\r### Expectation -- Discrete type\r\r- Let $X_1$ and $X_2$ be random variables of the discrete type with the joint pmf $f(x_1, x_2)$ on the space $S$.\r\r- If $u(X_1, X_2)$ is a function of $X_1$ and $X_2$, then $$E[u(X_1, X_2)] = \\sum\\sum_{(x_1,x_2)\\in S} u(x_1, x_2) f(x_1, x_2),$$ if it exists, is called the mathematical expectation of $u(X_1, X_2)$.\r\r### Remark\r\r- If $$\\sum\\sum_{(x_1,x_2)\\in S} |u(x_1, x_2)| f(x_1, x_2),$$ converges, then $E[u(X_1, X_2)]$ exists.\r\r### Expectation -- Continuous type\r\r- Let $X_1$ and $X_2$ be random variables of the discrete type with the joint pdf $f(x_1, x_2)$ on the space $S$.\r\r- If $u(X_1, X_2)$ is a function of $X_1$ and $X_2$, then $$E[u(X_1, X_2)] = \\int\\int_{(x_1,x_2)\\in S} u(x_1, x_2) f(x_1, x_2) dx_1dx_2,$$ if it exists, is called the mathematical expectation of $u(X_1, X_2)$.\r\r### Remark\r\r- If $$\\int\\int_{(x_1,x_2)\\in S} |u(x_1, x_2)| f(x_1, x_2)dx_1dx_2\u003c\\infty,$$ converges, then $E[u(X_1, X_2)]$ exists.\r\r### Expectation\r\r::: {#thm-ch2-1}\r- Let $(X_1, X_2)$ be a random vector.\r\r- Let $Y_1 = g_1(X_1, X_2)$ and $Y_2 = g_2(X_1,X_2)$ be random variables whose expectations exist.\r\r- For all real numbers $k_1$ and $k_2$, we have $$E[k_1Y_1 + k_2Y_2] = k_1E[Y_1] + k_2E[Y_2].$$\r:::\r\r### Moment generating function\r\r::: {#def-ch2-4}\r- Let ${\\boldsymbol X} = (X_1,X_2)^{'}$ be a random vector.\r\r- If $E[e^{t_1X_1+t_2X_2}]$ exists for $|t_1| \u003c h_1$ and $|t_2| \u003c h_2$, where $h_1$ and $h_2$ are positive.\r\r- It is denoted by $M_{X_1,X_2}(t_1,t_2)$ and is called the moment generating function (mgf) of ${\\boldsymbol X}$.\r:::\r\r### Note\r\r- Let ${\\boldsymbol t} = (t_1,t_2)^{'}$.\r\r- Then we can write the mgf of ${\\boldsymbol X}$ as $$M_{X_1,X_2} (t_1, t_2) = E[e^{{\\boldsymbol t}^{'}{\\boldsymbol X}}].$$\r\r- The mgfs of $X_1$ and $X_2$ are immediately seen to be $M_{X_1,X_2} (t_1, 0)$ and $M_{X_1,X_2} (0, t_2)$.\r\r### Example 9 （對應課本 Example 2.1.7）\r\r- Let $X$ and $Y$ have the joint pdf$$f(x,y) = \\left\\{\\begin{array}{ll}\r  e^{-y} & 0\u003cx\u003cy\u003c\\infty\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\\right..$$\r\r- Find $M(t_1, t_2)$, $M(t_1,0)$ and $M(0, t_2)$.\r\r### Expected value\r\r::: {#def-ch2-5}\r- Let ${\\boldsymbol X} = (X_1,X_2)^{'}$ be a random vector.\r\r- Then the expected value of ${\\boldsymbol X}$ exists if the expectations of $X_1$ and $X_2$ exist.\r\r- If it exists, then the expected value is given by $$E[{\\boldsymbol X}] = \\begin{bmatrix}\r  E[X_1]\\\\E[X_2]\r  \\end{bmatrix}$$\r:::\r\r## Transformations: Bivariate random variables\r\r### Transformations -- Discrete\r\r- Let $p_{X_1,X_2}(x_1,x_2)$ be the joint pmf of two discrete-type random variables $X_1$ and $X_2$ with the support ${\\mathcal{S}}$.\r\r- Let $y_1 = u_1(x_1,x_2)$ and $y_2 = u_2(x_1,x_2)$ define a one-to-one transformation that maps ${\\mathcal{S}}$ onto ${\\mathcal{T}}$ .\r\r- The joint pmf of the two new random variables $Y_1 = u_1(X_1,X_2)$ and $Y_2 = u_2(X_1,X_2)$ is given by $$\\begin{align*}\r  p_{Y_1, Y_2}(y_1, y_2) = \\left\\{\r  \\begin{array}{ll}\r  p_{X_1, X_2}(\\omega_1(y_1, y_2), \\omega_2(y_1, y_2)),&~(y_1, y_2)\\in {\\mathcal{T}}\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere},\r  \\end{array}\\right .\r  \\end{align*}$$ where $x_1 = \\omega_1(y_1, y_2)$, $x_2=\\omega_2(y_1, y_2)$ is the single-valued inverse of $y_1 = u_1(x_1,x_2)$ , $y_2 = u_2(x_1,x_2)$.\r\r### Example 10 （對應課本 Example 2.2.2）\r\r- Consider an experiment in which a person chooses at random a point $(X_1,X_2)$ from the unit square ${\\mathcal{S}} = {(x_1,x_2): 0 \u003c x_1 \u003c 1, 0 \u003c x_2 \u003c 1}$.\r\r- Suppose that our interest is not in $X_1$ or in $X_2$, but in $Z = X_1 +X_2$.\r\r- Find the pdf of $Z$.\r\r### Continuous transformation\r\r- Let $(X_1, X_2)$ have a jointly continuous distribution with pdf $f_{X_1,X_2} (x_1, x_2)$ and support set ${\\mathcal{S}}$.\r\r- Consider the transformed random vector $(Y_1, Y_2) = T (X_1, X_2)$ where $T$ is a one-to-one continuous transformation.\r\r- Let $T = T({\\mathcal{S}})$ denote the support of $(Y_1,Y_2)$.\r\r- Rewrite the transformation in terms of its components as $$(Y_1, Y_2) = T (X_1, X_2) = (u_1(X_1, X_2), u2(X_1, X_2)),$$ where the functions $y_1 = u_1(x_1, x_2)$ and $y_2 = u_2(x_1, x_2)$ define $T$.\r\r### Continuous transformation\r\r- Since the transformation is one-to-one, the inverse transformation $T^{-1}$ exists.\r\r- Write $x_1 = w_1(y_1, y_2)$, $x_2 = w_2(y_1, y_2)$.\r\r- Define the Jacobian of the transformation which is the determinant of order 2 given by $$J = \\left | \\begin{array}{cc}\r  \\frac{\\partial x_1}{\\partial y_1} &\r  \\frac{\\partial x_1}{\\partial y_2}\\\\\r  \\frac{\\partial x_2}{\\partial y_1} &\r  \\frac{\\partial x_2}{\\partial y_2}\\\\\r  \\end{array}\\right |$$\r\r### Continuous transformation\r\r::: columns\r- Let $B$ be any region in ${T}$.\r\r- Let $A = T^{-1}(B)$.\r\r- Because the transformation $T$ is one-to-one, $$\\begin{aligned}\r  \\lefteqn{P[(X_1,X_2) \\in A]}\\\\\r   &= P[T(X_1,X_2) \\in T(A)] \\\\\r   &= P[(Y_1,Y_2) \\in B].\r  \\end{aligned}$$\r\r![](assets/graph/fig2-2-1.png)\r:::\r\r### Continuous transformation\r\r- Based on the change-in-variable technique, we have $$\\begin{aligned}\r  P[(X_1,X_2)\\in A] & = \\int\\int_A f_{X_1, X_2}(x_1, x_2) dx_1 dx_2\\\\\r  &=\\int\\int_{T(A)} f_{X_1, X_2}(T^{-1}(y_1, y_2)) |J|dy_1 dy_2\\\\\r  &=\\int\\int_B f_{X_1, X_2}(w_1(y_1,y_2), w_2(y_1, y_2)) dy_1 dy_2\\\\\r  \\end{aligned}$$\r\r- The pdf of $(Y_1, Y_2)$ is $$f_{Y_1, Y_2}(y_1, y_2) =\r  \\left\\{\r  \\begin{array}{ll}\r  f_{X_1, X_2}(w_1(y_1,y_2), w_2(y_1, y_2)) |J|, & (y_1, y_2) \\in T\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\\right .$$\r\r### Example 11 （對應課本 Example 2.2.3）\r\r- Consider an experiment in which a person chooses at random a point $(X_1,X_2)$ from the unit square ${\\mathcal{S}} = \\{(x_1,x_2) : 0 \u003c x_1 \u003c 1, 0 \u003c x_2 \u003c 1\\}$.\r\r- Find the joint pdf of $Y_1 = X_1+X_2$ and $Y_2 = X_1-X_2$.\r\r### Example 13 （對應課本 Example 2.2.5）\r\r- Let $X_1$ and $X_2$ have the joint pdf $$f_{X_1, X_2}(x_1, x_2) =\r  \\left\\{\r  \\begin{array}{ll}\r  10x_1x_2^2, & 0\u003cx_1\u003cx_2\u003c1\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\\right .$$\r\r- Find the pdf of $Y_1 = X_1/X_2$.\r\r## Conditional Distributions and Expectations\r\r### Conditional distribution for discrete r.v.\r\r- Let $X_1$ and $X_2$ denote random variables of the discrete type.\r\r- Let the joint pmf be denoted as $p_{X_1,X_2}(x_1,x_2)$ that is positive on the support set ${\\mathcal{S}}$ and is zero elsewhere.\r\r- Let $p_{X_1}(x_1)$ and $p_{X_2}(x_2)$ denote, respectively, the marginal probability mass functions of $X_1$ and $X_2$.\r\r- Let $x_1$ be a point in the support of $X_1$, i.e., $p_{X_1} (x_1) > 0$.\r\r- Using the definition of conditional probability, we have $$\\begin{aligned}\r  p_{X_2|X_1}(x_2|x_1) =P[X_2=x_2|X_1=x_2] =& \\frac{P[X_1=x_1, X_2=x_2]}{P[X_1=x_1]} \\\\=& \\frac{p_{X_1, X_2}(x_1, x_2)}{p_{X_1}(x_1)}\r  \\end{aligned}$$ for all $x_1\\in {\\mathcal{S}}_{X_2}$.\r\r### Properties\r\r- $\\sum_{x_2} p_{X_2|X_1}(x_2|x_1) =1$.\r\r- $p_{X_2|X_1}(x_2|x_1)>0$ for all $x_2\\in {\\mathcal{S}}_{X_2}$.\r\r- $p_{X_2|X_1}(x_2|x_1)$ is called the conditional pmf of the discrete type of random variable.\r\r### Conditional distribution for continuous r.v.\r\r- Let $X_1$ and $X_2$ denote random variables of the continuous type.\r\r- Let the joint pdf be denoted as $f_{X_1,X_2}(x_1,x_2)$ that is positive on the support set ${\\mathcal{S}}$ and is zero elsewhere.\r\r- Let $f_{X_1}(x_1)$ and $f_{X_2}(x_2)$ denote, respectively, the conditional probability density functions of $X_1$ and $X_2$.\r\r- When $f_{X_1} (x_1) > 0$, we define $$\\begin{aligned}\r  f_{X_2|X_1}(x_2|x_1) =& \\frac{f_{X_1, X_2}(x_1, x_2)}{f_{X_1}(x_1)}.\r  \\end{aligned}$$\r\r### Properties\r\r- $\\int_{-\\infty}^{\\infty} f_{X_2|X_1}(x_2|x_1) dx_2=1$.\r\r- $f_{X_2|X_1}(x_2|x_1)>0$ for all $x_2\\in {\\mathcal{S}}_{X_2}$.\r\r- $f_{X_2|X_1}(x_2|x_1)$ is called the conditional pdf of the continuous type of random variable.\r\r### Conditional probability of continuous r.v.\r\r- If the random variables are of the continuous type, the conditional probability that $a \u003c X_2 \u003c b$, given that $X_1 = x_1$, is $$\\begin{align*}\r  P[a\u003cX_2\u003cb|X_1=x_1] = \\int_{a}^{b} f_{X_2|X_1}(x_2|x_1)dx_2\r  \\end{align*}$$\r\r### Conditional expectation of r.v.\r\r- If $u(X_2)$ is a function of $X_2$, the conditional expectation of $u(X_2)$, given that $X_1 = x_1$, if it exists, is given by $$\\begin{align*}\r  E[u(X_2)|X_1=x_1] = \\left\\{\r  \\begin{array}{ll}\r  \\sum_{x_2} u(x_2) p_{X_2|X_1}(x_2|x_1), &\\text{Discrete}\\\\\r  \\int_{x_2} u(x_2) f_{X_2|X_1}(x_2|x_1) dx_2, &\\text{Continuous}\r  \\end{array}\\right .\r  \\end{align*}$$\r\r- The conditional variance of $X_2$, given that $X_1 = x_1$, if it exists, is given by $$\\begin{align*}\r  \\operatorname{Var}(X_2|x_1) =& E[(X_2 - E[X_2|x_1])^2|X_1=x_1] \\\\=& E[X_2^2|x_1] - (E[X_2|x_1])^2\r  \\end{align*}$$\r\r### Example 16 （對應課本 Example 2.3.1）\r\r- Let $X_1$ and $X_2$ have the joint pdf $$f_{X_1, X_2}(x_1, x_2) =\r  \\left\\{\r  \\begin{array}{ll}\r  2, & 0\u003cx_1\u003cx_2\u003c1\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\\right .$$\r\r- Find $f_{X_1|X_2}(x_1|x_2)$ and $f_{X_2|X_1}(x_2|x_1)$.\r\r### Example 16 (continued) （對應課本 Example 2.3.1）\r\r- Let $X_1$ and $X_2$ have the joint pdf $$f_{X_1, X_2}(x_1, x_2) =\r  \\left\\{\r  \\begin{array}{ll}\r  2, & 0\u003cx_1\u003cx_2\u003c1\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\\right .$$\r\r- Find $E[X_1|x_2]$ and $\\operatorname{Var}(X_1|x_2)$.\r\r### Example 17 （沿用課本 Example 2.3.1 情境的補充計算，查無獨立編號）\r\r- Let $X_1$ and $X_2$ have the joint pdf $$f_{X_1, X_2}(x_1, x_2) =\r  \\left\\{\r  \\begin{array}{ll}\r  2, & 0\u003cx_1\u003cx_2\u003c1\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\\right .$$\r\r- Find $P[0\u003cX_1\u003c\\frac{1}{2}|X_2 =\\frac{3}{4}]$ and $P[0\u003cX_1\u003c\\frac{1}{2}]$\r\r### Conditional variance and variance\r\r::: {#thm-ch2-2}\r- Let $(X_1,X_2)$ be a random vector such that the variance of $X_2$ is finite.\r\r- We have\r\r  - $E[E[X_2|X_1]] = E[X_2]$.\r\r  - $\\operatorname{Var}(E[X_2|X_1]) \\le \\operatorname{Var}(X_2)$.\r:::\r\r## Independent random variables\r\r### Independence\r\r::: {#def-ch2-6}\r- Let the random variables $X_1$ and $X_2$ have the joint pdf $f(x_1,x_2)$ \\[joint pmf $p(x_1,x_2)$\\].\r\r- Let the marginal pdfs \\[pmfs\\] $f_1(x_1) [p_1(x_1)]$ and $f_2(x_2) [p_2(x_2)]$.\r\r- $X_1$ and $X_2$ are said to be independent if, and only if, $$f(x_1, x_2) \\equiv f_1(x_1)f_2(x_2) [p(x_1, x_2) \\equiv p_1(x_1)p_2(x_2)].$$\r\r- Random variables that are not independent are said to be dependent.\r:::\r\r### Remark\r\r- The product of two positive functions $f_1(x_1)f_2(x_2)$ means a function that is positive on the product space.\r\r- There may be certain points $(x_1,x_2) \\in {\\mathcal{S}}$ at which $f(x_1,x_2) \\ne f_1(x_1)f_2(x_2)$. However, if $A$ is the set of points $(x_1,x_2)$ at which the equality does not hold, then $P(A) = 0$.\r\r### Example 18 （查無 HMC 7e 直接對應例題，屬補充教材）\r\r- Suppose an urn contains 10 blue, 8 red, and 7 yellow balls that are the same except for color.\r\r- Suppose 4 balls are drawn without replacement.\r\r- Let $X$ and $Y$ be the number of red and blue balls drawn.\r\r- Are $X$ and $Y$ independent?\r\r### Independence\r\r::: {#thm-ch2-3}\r- Let the random variables $X_1$ and $X_2$ have supports ${\\mathcal{S}}_1$ and ${\\mathcal{S}}_2$, respectively, and have the joint pdf $f(x_1,x_2)$.\r\r- Then $X_1$ and $X_2$ are independent if and only if $f(x_1,x_2)$ can be written as a product of a nonnegative function of $x_1$ and a nonnegative function of $x_2$.\r\r- That is, $$f(x_1,x_2) ≡ g(x_1)h(x_2),$$ where $g(x_1) > 0$, $x_1\\in {\\mathcal{S}}_1$, zero elsewhere, and $h(x_2) > 0$, $x_2\\in {\\mathcal{S}}_2$, zero elsewhere.\r:::\r\r### Example 20 （對應 2.1 節內文範例，非獨立編號 Example）\r\r- Let $X_1$ and $X_2$ have the joint pdf $$f_{X_1, X_2}(x_1, x_2) =\r  \\left\\{\r  \\begin{array}{ll}\r  4xe^{-x^2}ye^{-y^2}, & x>0, y>0\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\\right .$$\r\r- Are $X_1$ and $X_2$ independent?\r\r### Example 21 （對應課本 Example 2.5.2）\r\r- Let $X_1$ and $X_2$ have the joint pdf $$f_{X_1, X_2}(x_1, x_2) =\r  \\left\\{\r  \\begin{array}{ll}\r  8x_1x_2 & 0\u003cx_1\u003cx_2\u003c1\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\\right .$$\r\r- Are $X$ and $Y$ independent?\r\r### Independence\r\r::: {#thm-ch2-4}\r- Let $X_1$ and $X_2$ have the joint cdf $F(x_1,x_2)$.\r\r- Let $X_1$ and $X_2$ have the marginal cdfs $F_1(x_1)$ and $F_2(x_2)$.\r\r- Then $X_1$ and $X_2$ are independent if and only if $$F(x_1,x_2) = F_1(x_1) F_2(x_2)$$ for all $(x_1, x_2) \\in R^{2}$.\r:::\r\r### Independence\r\r::: {#thm-ch2-5}\r- $X_1$ and $X_2$ are independent if and only if $$P[a\u003cX_1\\le b, c\u003cX_2\\le d] = P[a\u003cX_1\\le b]P[c\u003cX_2\\le d]$$ for every $a\u003cb$ and $c\u003cd$, where $a, b, c, d$ are constants.\r:::\r\r### Independence\r\r::: {#thm-ch2-6}\r- Suppose $X_1$ and $X_2$ are independent.\r\r- Suppose $E[u(X_1)]\u003c\\infty$ and $E[\\nu(X_2)]\u003c\\infty$.\r\r- We have $$E[u(X_1)\\nu(X_2)] = E[u(X_1)]E[\\nu(X_2)].$$\r:::\r\r### Independence\r\r::: {#thm-ch2-7}\r- Suppose $M(t_1, t_2)\u003c\\infty$.\r\r- Then $X_1$ and $X_2$ are independent if and only if $$M(t_1, t_2) = M(t_1, 0) M(0, t_2).$$\r:::\r\r### Example 23 （對應課本 Example 2.5.5）\r\r- Let $X_1$ and $X_2$ have the joint pdf $$f_{X_1, X_2}(x_1, x_2) =\r  \\left\\{\r  \\begin{array}{ll}\r  e^{-x_2} & 0\u003cx_1\u003cx_2\u003c\\infty\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\\right .$$\r\r- Find $M(t_1, t_2)$.\r\r- Show that $M(t_1, t_2) \\ne M(t_1, 0) M(0, t_2).$\r\r## The correlation coefficient\r\r### Covariance\r\r::: {#def-ch2-7}\r- Let $(X, Y)$ have a joint distribution.\r\r- Denote the means of $X$ and $Y$ by $\\mu_1$ and $\\mu_2$ and their respective variances by $\\sigma_1^2$ and $\\sigma_2^2$.\r\r- The covariance of $(X, Y)$ is denoted by cov($X$, $Y$) and is defined by the expectation $$\\operatorname{cov}(X, Y) = E[(X - \\mu_1)(Y - \\mu_2)].$$\r:::\r\r### Note\r\r- The covariance of $X$ and $Y$ can also be expressed as $$\\operatorname{cov}(X, Y) = E[XY] - \\mu_1\\mu_2.$$\r\r### Correlation coefficient\r\r::: {#def-ch2-8}\r- If each of $\\sigma_1$ and $\\sigma_2$ is positive, then the correlation coefficient between $X$ and $Y$ is defined by $$\\rho = \\frac{\\operatorname{cov}(X, Y)}{\\sigma_1\\sigma_2}.$$\r:::\r\r### Note\r\r- The expected value of the product of two random variables is equal to the product of their expectations plus their covariance; $$E[XY] = \\mu_1\\mu_2 + \\operatorname{Cov}(X, Y) = \\mu_1\\mu_2 + \\rho\\sigma_1\\sigma_2.$$\r\r### Example 24 （情境沿用課本 2.1 節開頭範例，該處未計算相關係數，查無獨立編號）\r\r- A fair coin is flipped three times.\r\r- $X_1$ is the number of heads on the first two flips while $X_2$ is the number of heads on all three flips.\r\r- Find the correlation coefficient of $(X_1,X_2)$.\r\r### Correlation coefficient[^1]\r\r::: {#thm-ch2-8}\r- For all jointly distributed random variables $(X,Y)$ whose correlation coefficient $\\rho$ exists, $-1 \\le \\rho\\le 1$.\r:::\r\r### Note\r\r- A quadratic equation is said positive when its discriminant is negative.\r\r- Define the quadratic equation as $y = ax^2+bx+c$.\r\r- The discriminant is $b^2-4ac$.\r\r### Independence\r\r::: {#thm-ch2-9}\r- If $X$ and $Y$ are independent random variables, then $$\\rho = 0.$$\r:::\r\r### Example 26 （概念對應課本 Exercise 2.5.10，座標點不同）\r\r- Let $X$ and $Y$ be jointly discrete random variables whose distribution has mass 1/4 at each of the four points (-1, 0), (0, -1), (1, 0) and (0, 1).\r\r- Find the correlation coefficient of $(X_1,X_2)$.\r\r- Are $X$ and $Y$ independent?\r\r### Linear function\r\r::: {#thm-ch2-10}\r- Suppose $(X, Y)$ have a joint distribution with the variances of $X$ and $Y$ finite and positive.\r\r- Denote the means and variances of $X$ and $Y$ by $\\mu_1$, $\\mu_2$ and $\\sigma_1^2$, $\\sigma_2^2$.\r\r- Let $\\rho$ be the correlation coefficient between $X$ and $Y$.\r\r- If $E[Y|X]$ is linear in $X$ then $$E[Y|X] = \\mu_2 + \\rho\\frac{\\sigma_2}{\\sigma_1}(X-\\mu_1)$$ and $$E[\\operatorname{Var}(Y|X)] = \\sigma_2^2 (1-\\rho^2).$$\r:::\r\r### Example 27 （對應課本 Example 2.4.2）\r\r- Let the random variables X and Y have the linear conditional means $E(Y |x) = 4x + 3$ and $E(X|y) = \\frac{1}{16} y - 3$.\r\r- Can $\\mu_1$, $\\mu_2$, $\\rho$, $\\sigma_1$ and $\\sigma_2$ be obtained?\r\r## Extension to several random variables\r\r### Random vector\r\r::: {#def-ch2-9}\r- Consider a random experiment with a sample space ${\\mathcal{C}}$.\r\r- Let the random variable $X_i$ assign to each element $c \\in {\\mathcal{C}}$ one and only one real number $X_i(c) = x_i$, $i = 1,2,...,n$.\r\r- Then,\r\r  - ${\\boldsymbol X} = (X_1,X_2, \\cdots, X_n)$ is a $n$-dimensional random vector.\r\r  - The space of ${\\boldsymbol X}$ is the set of ordered $n$-tuples $${\\mathcal{D}} = \\{(x_1, x_2, \\ldots, x_n) : x_1 = X_1(c), \\ldots, x_n = X_n(c), c \\in {\\mathcal{C}}\\}.$$\r\r- Furthermore, let $A$ be a subset of the space ${\\mathcal{D}}$. Then $P[(X_1,\\cdots,X_n) \\in A] = P[C]$, where $C = \\{c: c \\in {\\mathcal{C}}\\}$ and $(X_1(c), X_2(c), \\cdots, X_n(c)) \\in A$.\r:::\r\r### Cumulative distribution function (cdf)\r\r- Let $A\\subset {\\mathcal{D}}$.\r\r- Denote the probability of the event $A$ as $P_{X_1, X_2, \\ldots, X_n}[A]$.\r\r- $P_{X_1, X_2, \\ldots, X_n}[A]$ is uniquely determined by the cumulative distribution function (cdf), which is given by $$F_{\\boldsymbol X}(x_1,x_2, \\ldots, x_n)=P[X_1 \\le x_1, \\ldots, X_n \\le x_n],$$ for all $(x_1, x_2,\\ldots, x_n)\\in R^n$.\r\r### Cumulative distribution function (cdf)\r\r- Discrete random vector $$F_{\\boldsymbol X}(x_1,x_2, \\ldots, x_n)=\\sum\\cdots\\sum_{\\omega_1\\le x_1,\\ldots,\\omega_n\\le x_n}p(\\omega_1, \\ldots, \\omega_n)$$\r\r- Continuous random vector $$F_{\\boldsymbol X}(x_1,x_2, \\ldots, x_n)=\\int_{-\\infty}^{x_1}\\cdots\\int_{-\\infty}^{x_n}f(\\omega_1, \\ldots, \\omega_n)d\\omega\\cdots d\\omega_n.$$ and $$\\frac{\\partial^n}{\\partial x_1\\cdots\\partial x_n}F_{\\boldsymbol X} ({\\boldsymbol x}) = f({\\boldsymbol x}).$$\r\r### Example 30 （對應課本 Example 2.6.1）\r\r- Let the random variables $X$, $Y$ and $Z$ have the joint pdf defined as $$f(x, y, z) =\r  \\left\\{\r  \\begin{array}{ll}\r  e^{-x+y+z} & 0\u003cx,y, z\u003c\\infty\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\\right .$$\r\r- Find the distribution funtion of $X$, $Y$ and $Z$.\r\r### Expectation for discrete random vector\r\r- Let ${\\boldsymbol X} = (X_1, X_2, \\ldots, X_n)$ be a random vector.\r\r- Let $Y = u(X_1, X_2, \\ldots, X_n)$ for some function $u$.\r\r- Then $$\\begin{aligned}\r  \\lefteqn{E[u(X_1, X_2, \\ldots, X_n)]}\\\\\r  & = \\sum\\cdots\\sum_{(x_1,\\ldots, x_n)\\in {\\mathcal{D}}} u(x_1, \\ldots, x_n) f(x_1, \\ldots, x_n),\r  \\end{aligned}$$ if it exists, is called the mathematical expectation of $u(X_1, \\ldots, X_n)$.\r\r### Expectation for continuous random vector\r\r- Let ${\\boldsymbol X} = (X_1, X_2, \\ldots, X_n)$ be a random vector.\r\r- Let $Y = u(X_1, X_2, \\ldots, X_n)$ for some function $u$.\r\r- Then $$\\begin{aligned}\r  &\\lefteqn{E[u(X_1, \\ldots, X_n)]}  \\\\\r  &= \\int\\cdots\\int_{(x_1,\\ldots, x_n)\\in {\\mathcal{D}}} u(x_1, \\ldots, x_n) f(x_1, \\ldots, x_n) dx_1\\cdots dx_n,\r  \\end{aligned}$$ if it exists, is called the mathematical expectation of $u(X_1, \\ldots, X_n)$.\r\r### Marginal pmf\r\r- Let ($X_1$, $\\ldots$, $X_n$) be a random vector.\r\r- Find the pmf of $X_1$.\r\r### Note\r\r- Take any group of $k \u003c n$ of these random variables and find the joint pmf of them.\r\r- This joint pmf is called the marginal pmf of this particular group of $k$ variables.\r\r### Marginal pdf\r\r- Let ($X_1$, $\\ldots$, $X_n$) be a random vector.\r\r- Find the probability of $\\{X_1\\le x_1\\}$ and the pdf of $X_1$.\r\r### Note\r\r- Take any group of $k \u003c n$ of these random variables and find the joint pdf of them.\r\r- This joint pdf is called the marginal pdf of this particular group of $k$ variables.\r\r### Conditional distribution for discrete type\r\r- Let $x_1$ be a point in the support of $X_1$, i.e., $p_{X_1} (x_1) > 0$.\r\r- Using the definition of conditional probability, we have $$\\begin{aligned}\r  p_{X_2, \\ldots, X_n|X_1}(x_2, \\cdots, x_n|x_1) =&P[X_2=x_2, \\cdots, X_n=x_n|X_1=x_1]  \\\\=& \\frac{p_{\\boldsymbol X}(x_1, x_2, \\cdots, x_n)}{p_{X_1}(x_1)}.\r  \\end{aligned}$$\r\r### Conditional distribution for continuous type\r\r- When $f_{X_1} (x_1) > 0$, we define $$\\begin{aligned}\r  f_{X_2, \\ldots, X_n|X_1}(x_2, \\cdots, x_n|x_1) =& \\frac{f_{X_1, X_2}(x_1, x_2, \\cdots, x_n)}{f_{X_1}(x_1)}.\r  \\end{aligned}$$\r\r- The joint conditional pdf of any $n - 1$ random variables, say $X_1,\\cdots,X_{i-1},X_{i+1},\\cdots,X_n$, given $X_i = x_i$, is defined as the joint pdf of $X_1,\\cdots,X_n$ divided by the marginal pdf $f_{X_i}(x_i)$, provided that $f_{X_i}(x_i) > 0$.\r\r### Conditional expectation of a discrete random vector\r\r- If $u(X_2, \\cdots, X_n)$ is a function of $X_2$, $\\ldots$, $X_n$, the conditional expectation of $u(X_2, \\cdots, X_n)$, given that $X_1 = x_1$, if it exists, is given by\r\r$$\\begin{align*}\r\\lefteqn{E[u(X_2, \\cdots, X_n)|X_1=x_1] }\\\\\r&=\r\\sum\\cdots\\sum_{x_2, \\cdots, x_n} u(x_2, \\cdots, x_n) p_{X_2, \\ldots, X_n|X_1}(x_2, \\cdots, x_n|x_1),\r\\end{align*}$$\r\r### Conditional expectation of a continuous random vector\r\r- If $u(X_2, \\cdots, X_n)$ is a function of $X_2$, $\\ldots$, $X_n$, the conditional expectation of $u(X_2, \\cdots, X_n)$, given that $X_1 = x_1$, if it exists, is given by\r\r$$\\begin{align*}\r\\lefteqn{E[u(X_2, \\cdots, X_n)|X_1=x_1] }\\\\\r&= \r\\int\\cdots\\int_{x_2, \\cdots, x_n} u(x_2, \\cdots, x_n) f_{X_2\\ldots, X_n|X_1}(x_2, \\cdots, x_n|x_1) dx_2\\cdots dx_n,\r\\end{align*}$$\r\r### Independence\r\r::: {#def-ch2-10}\r- Let the random variables $X_1, \\cdots, X_n$ have the joint pdf $f(x_1,\\cdots, x_n)$ \\[joint pmf $p(x_1,\\cdots, x_n)$\\].\r\r- Let the marginal pdfs \\[pmfs\\] $f_1(x_1) [p_1(x_1)]$, $\\cdots$, $f_n(x_n) [p_n(x_n)]$.\r\r- $X_1, \\cdots, X_n$ are said to be mutually independent if, and only if, $$f(x_1,\\cdots, x_n) \\equiv f_1(x_1)\\cdots f_n(x_n) [p(x_1, \\cdots, x_n) \\equiv p_1(x_1)\\cdots p_n(x_n)].$$\r\r- Random variables that are not independent are said to be dependent.\r:::\r\r### Independence\r\r::: {#thm-ch2-11}\r- Suppose $X_1, \\cdots, X_n$ are independent.\r\r- Suppose $E[u(X_i)]\u003c\\infty$, $i=1,2, \\ldots, n$.\r\r- We have $$E[u(X_1)\\cdots u(X_n)] = E[u(X_1)]\\cdots E[u(X_n)] = \\prod_{i=1}^n E[u(X_i)]$$\r:::\r\r### Moment generating function\r\r::: {#def-ch2-11}\r- Let ${\\boldsymbol X} = (X_1,X_2, \\cdots, X_n)$ be a random vector.\r\r- If $E[e^{\\sum_{i=1}^nt_iX_i}]$ exists for $|t_i| \u003c h_i$, $i=1,\\ldots, n$ where $h_i>0$, $i=1,\\ldots, n$.\r\r- It is denoted by $M_{\\boldsymbol X}(t_1,\\cdots, t_n)$ and is called the moment generating function (mgf) of ${\\boldsymbol X}$.\r:::\r\r### Independence\r\r::: {#thm-ch2-12}\r- Suppose $X_1, X_2, \\cdots, X_n$ are $n$ mutually independent random variables.\r\r- Suppose, for all $i = 1,2,\\cdots,n$, $X_i$ has mgf $M_i(t)$, for $|t| \u003c h_i$, where $h_i > 0$.\r\r- Let $T = \\sum_{i=1}^n k_iX_i$, where $k_1,k_2,\\ldots, k_n$ are constants.\r\r- Then $T$ has the mgf given by $$M_T(t) = \\prod_{i=1}^n M_i(k_it),$$ where $-\\min_i (h_i) \u003c t \u003c\\min_i{h_i}$.\r:::\r\r### Example 31 （查無 HMC 7e 直接對應例題，屬補充教材）\r\r- Let the random variables $X_1$, $X_2$ and $X_3$ be three mutually independent random variable and let each have the pdf $$f(x) =\r  \\left\\{\r  \\begin{array}{ll}\r  2x & 0\u003cx\u003c1\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\\right .$$\r\r- Find the distribution function and pdf of $Y=\\max_i X_i$.\r\r### Note\r\r- Unless there is a possible misunderstanding between mutual and pairwise independence, we usually drop the modifier mutual.\r\r- If several random variables are mutually independent and have the same distribution, we say that they are independent and identically distributed, which we abbreviate as iid.\r\r### Independence\r\r::: corollary\r- Suppose $X_1, X_2, \\cdots, X_n$ are iid random variable with the common mgf $M(t)$, for $|t| \u003c h$, where $h > 0$.\r\r- Let $T = \\sum_{i=1}^n X_i$.\r\r- Then $T$ has the mgf given by $$M_T(t) = [M(t)]^n,$$ where $|t| \u003c h$.\r:::\r\r### Random matrix\r\r- Let ${\\boldsymbol X} = (X_1, \\ldots, X_n)^{'}$ be an $n$-dimensional random vector.\r\r- Define $E({\\boldsymbol X}) = (E[X_1], \\ldots, E[X_n])^{'}$.\r\r- Suppose ${\\boldsymbol W}$ is an $m \\times n$ matrix of random variables with the $ij$th component $W_{ij}$, $1 \\le i \\le m$ and $1 \\le j \\le n$.\r\r- Define the expectation of a random matrix $$E[{\\boldsymbol W}] = [E(W_{ij})].$$\r\r### Random matrix\r\r::: {#thm-ch2-13}\r- Let ${\\boldsymbol W}_1$ and ${\\boldsymbol W}_2$ be $m \\times n$ matrices of random variables.\r\r- Let ${\\boldsymbol A}_1$ and ${\\boldsymbol A}_2$ be $k \\times m$ matrices of constants.\r\r- Let ${\\boldsymbol B}$ be an $n\\times l$ matrix of constants.\r\r- Then, we have\r\r  - $E[{\\boldsymbol A}_1{\\boldsymbol W}_1+{\\boldsymbol A}_2{\\boldsymbol W}_2] = {\\boldsymbol A}_1E[{\\boldsymbol W}_1] +{\\boldsymbol A}_2E[{\\boldsymbol W}_2].$\r\r  - $E[{\\boldsymbol A}_1{\\boldsymbol W}_1{\\boldsymbol B}] = {\\boldsymbol A}_1E[{\\boldsymbol W}_1]{\\boldsymbol B}$\r:::\r\r### Random vector\r\r- Let ${\\boldsymbol X} = (X_1,\\ldots, X_n)^{'}$ be an $n$-dimensional random vector, such that $\\sigma_i^2=\\sigma_{ii}=\\operatorname{Var}(X_i)\u003c\\infty$.\r\r- The mean of ${\\boldsymbol X}$ is $\\boldsymbol{\\mu} = E[\\boldsymbol{X}]$. and we define its variance-covariance matrix as $$\\operatorname{Cov}({\\boldsymbol X}) = E[({\\boldsymbol X} - {\\boldsymbol \\mu})({\\boldsymbol X} - {\\boldsymbol \\mu})^{'}] = [\\sigma_{ij}]$$ whose the ($i$,$j$)th off diagonal entry is $\\operatorname{Cov}(X_i,X_j)$ and the $(i, i)$th diagonal entry is $\\sigma_i^2 = \\operatorname{Var}(X_i)$.\r\r### Example 32 （對應課本 Example 2.6.3）\r\r- Let the random variables $X$ and $Y$ have the joint pdf of $X$ and $Y$ is $$f_{X, Y}(x, y) =\r  \\left\\{\r  \\begin{array}{ll}\r  e^{-y} & 0\u003cx\u003cy\u003c\\infty\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\\right .$$\r\r- Let ${\\boldsymbol Z} = (X, Y)^{'}$.\r\r- Find $E[{\\boldsymbol Z}]$ and $\\operatorname{Cov}({\\boldsymbol Z})$.\r\r### Random vector\r\r::: {#thm-ch2-14}\r- Let ${\\boldsymbol X} = (X_1,\\ldots, X_n)^{'}$ be an $n$-dimensional random vector, such that $\\sigma_i^2=\\sigma_{ii}=\\operatorname{Var}(X_i)\u003c\\infty$.\r\r- Let ${\\boldsymbol A}$ be an $m \\times n$ matrices of constants.\r\r- Then, we have\r\r  - $\\operatorname{Cov}({\\boldsymbol X}) = E[{\\boldsymbol X}{\\boldsymbol X}^{'}] - {\\boldsymbol \\mu} {\\boldsymbol \\mu}^{'}$\r\r  - $\\operatorname{Cov}({\\boldsymbol A}{\\boldsymbol X}) = {\\boldsymbol A}\\operatorname{Cov}({\\boldsymbol X}) {\\boldsymbol A}^{'}$.\r:::\r\r### Note\r\r- All variance-covariance matrices are positive semi-definite matrices; that is, $${\\boldsymbol a}^{'}\\operatorname{Cov}({\\boldsymbol X}){\\boldsymbol a} \\ge 0,$$ for all vectors ${\\boldsymbol a} \\in R^n$.\r\r- Let ${\\boldsymbol X}$ be a random vector.\r\r- Let ${\\boldsymbol a}$ be any $n \\times 1$ vector of constants.\r\r- Then ${Y}$ = ${\\boldsymbol a}^{'} {\\boldsymbol X}$ is a random variable and, hence, has nonnegative variance; i.e., $$0\\le \\operatorname{Var}(Y) = \\operatorname{Var}({\\boldsymbol a}^{'}\\boldsymbol{X}) =\r  {\\boldsymbol a}^{'}\\operatorname{Cov}({\\boldsymbol X}){\\boldsymbol a}.$$\r\r## Transformations for several random variables\r\r### Continuous transformation\r\r- Let $(X_1, \\ldots, X_n)$ have a jointly continuous distribution with pdf $f(x_1, \\cdots, x_n)$ and support set ${\\mathcal{S}}$.\r\r- Consider a one-to-one transformation that maps ${\\mathcal{S}}$ onto ${\\mathcal{T}}$.\r\r- Let $y_1 = u_1(x_1, x_2, \\ldots, x_n)$, $y_2 = u_2(x_1, x_2, \\ldots, x_n)$, $\\cdots$ and $y_n = u_n(x_1, x_2, \\ldots, x_n)$.\r\r- Let the inverse function be denoted as $$\\begin{aligned}\r  x_1 =& w_1(y_1, y_2, \\ldots, y_n)\\\\\r  x_2 =& w_2(y_1, y_2, \\ldots, y_n)\\\\\r  &\\vdots\\\\\r  x_n =& w_n(y_1, y_2, \\ldots, y_n)\r  \\end{aligned}.$$\r\r### Continuous transformation\r\r- Define the Jacobian of the transformation as $$J = \\left | \\begin{array}{cccc}\r  \\frac{\\partial x_1}{\\partial y_1} &\r  \\frac{\\partial x_1}{\\partial y_2} & \\cdots & \\frac{\\partial x_1}{\\partial y_n}\\\\\r  \\frac{\\partial x_2}{\\partial y_1} &\r  \\frac{\\partial x_2}{\\partial y_2} & \\cdots & \\frac{\\partial x_2}{\\partial y_n}\\\\\r  \\vdots & \\vdots & \\ddots & \\vdots\\\\\r  \\frac{\\partial x_n}{\\partial y_1} &\r  \\frac{\\partial x_n}{\\partial y_2} & \\cdots & \\frac{\\partial x_n}{\\partial y_n}\\\\\r  \\end{array}\\right |$$\r\r- $J$ can not be identically zero in ${\\mathcal{T}}$.\r\r### Continuous transformation\r\r- Based on the change-in-variable technique, we have $$\\begin{aligned}\r  \\lefteqn{\\int\\int_{A} f(x_1, x_2, \\ldots, x_n) dx_1 dx_2\\cdots dx_n}\\\\\r  &=\\int\\int_B f(w_1(y_1,y_2), w_2(y_1, y_2)) |J|dy_1 dy_2\\cdots dy_n\\\\\r  \\end{aligned}$$\r\r- The pdf of $(Y_1, Y_2, \\ldots, Y_n)$ is $$\\begin{aligned}\r  \\lefteqn{f(y_1, y_2, \\ldots, y_n)}\\\\\r  & =\r  \\left\\{\r  \\begin{array}{ll}\r  f(w_1(y_1,y_2, \\ldots, y_n), \\ldots,  w_n(y_1,y_2, \\ldots, y_n)) |J|, & {\\boldsymbol y} \\in {\\mathcal{T}}\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\\right .\r  \\end{aligned}$$\r\r### Example 33 （對應課本 Example 2.7.1）\r\r- Let $X_1$, $X_2$ and $X_3$ have the joint pdf $$f(x_1, x_2, x_3) =\r  \\left\\{\r  \\begin{array}{ll}\r  48 x_1 x_2 x_3 & 0\u003c x_1\u003cx_2\u003cx_3\u003c1\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\\right .$$\r\r- Find the joint pdf of $Y_1$, $Y_2$ and $Y_3$.\r\r- Find the marginal pdf of $Y_1$ and that of $Y_2$.\r\r### Example 35 — Many to one （對應 2.7 節內文說明（Cauchy 範例），非獨立編號 Example）\r\r- Let $X$ have the Cauchy pdf $$f(x) = \r  \\left\\{\r  \\begin{array}{ll}\r  \\frac{1}{\\pi(1+x^2)}, & -\\infty\u003cx\u003c\\infty\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\\right .$$\r\r- Let $Y = X^2$.\r\r- Find the pdf of $Y$.\r\r### Continuous transformation\r\r- Let $(X_1, \\ldots, X_n)$ have a jointly continuous distribution with pdf $f(x_1, \\cdots, x_n)$ and support set ${\\mathcal{S}}$.\r\r- Consider the transformation $y_1 = u_1(x_1, x_2, \\ldots, x_n)$, $y_2 = u_2(x_1, x_2, \\ldots, x_n)$, $\\cdots$ and $y_n = u_n(x_1, x_2, \\ldots, x_n)$, which maps ${\\mathcal{S}}$ to ${\\mathcal{T}}$.\r\r- To each point of ${\\mathcal{S}}$ there corresponds, of course, only one point in ${\\mathcal{T}}$; but to a point in ${\\mathcal{T}}$ there may correspond more than one point in ${\\mathcal{S}}$.\r\r### Continuous transformation\r\r- Suppose that we can represent ${\\mathcal{S}}$ as the union of a finite number, say $k$, of mutually disjoint sets $A_1$, $A_2$, $\\ldots$, $A_k$ so that $$y_1 = u_1(x_1,x_2,\\ldots,x_n),\\ldots,y_n = u_n(x_1,x_2,\\ldots,x_n)$$ define a one-to-one transformation of each $A_i$ onto ${\\mathcal{T}}$.\r\r- To each point in ${\\mathcal{T}}$, there corresponds exactly one point in each of $A_1$, $A_2$, $\\ldots$, $A_k$.\r\r- For $i = 1,\\ldots, k$, let the inverse function be denoted as $$\\begin{aligned}\r  x_1 =& w_{1i}(y_1, y_2, \\ldots, y_n)\\\\\r  x_2 =& w_{2i}(y_1, y_2, \\ldots, y_n)\\\\\r  &\\vdots\\\\\r  x_n =& w_{ni}(y_1, y_2, \\ldots, y_n)\r  \\end{aligned}.$$\r\r### Continuous transformation\r\r- Define the Jacobian of the transformation as $$J_i = \\left | \\begin{array}{cccc}\r  \\frac{\\partial x_{1i}}{\\partial y_1} &\r  \\frac{\\partial x_{1i}}{\\partial y_2} & \\cdots & \\frac{\\partial x_{1i}}{\\partial y_n}\\\\\r  \\frac{\\partial x_{2i}}{\\partial y_1} &\r  \\frac{\\partial x_{2i}}{\\partial y_2} & \\cdots & \\frac{\\partial x_{2i}}{\\partial y_n}\\\\\r  \\vdots & \\vdots & \\ddots & \\vdots\\\\\r  \\frac{\\partial x_{ni}}{\\partial y_1} &\r  \\frac{\\partial x_{ni}}{\\partial y_2} & \\cdots & \\frac{\\partial x_{ni}}{\\partial y_n}\\\\\r  \\end{array}\\right |, i = 1, \\ldots, k.$$\r\r- $J_i$, $i=1, \\ldots, k$, can not be identically zero in ${\\mathcal{T}}$.\r\r- The pdf of $(Y_1, Y_2, \\ldots, Y_n)$ is $$\\begin{aligned}\r  \\lefteqn{f(y_1, y_2, \\ldots, y_n)}\\\\\r  & =\r  \\sum_{i=1}^kf(w_{1i}(y_1,y_2, \\ldots, y_n), \\ldots,  w_{ni}(y_1,y_2, \\ldots, y_n)) |J_i|\r  \\end{aligned}$$ provided ${\\boldsymbol y} \\in {\\mathcal{T}}$.\r\r### Example 37 （對應課本 Example 2.7.5）\r\r- Let $X_1$, $X_2$, $X_3$, $X_4$ be independent random variables with com- mon pdf $$f(x) =\r  \\left\\{\r  \\begin{array}{ll}\r  e^{-x} & 0\u003cx\\\\\r  0 & \\text{elsewhere}\r  \\end{array}\\right .$$\r\r- Find the distribution function of $Y = \\sum_{i=1}^4X_i$ using the mgf technique.\r\r## Linear combinations random variables\r\r### Linear combination\r\r- Let ${\\boldsymbol X} = (X_1, \\ldots, X_n)^{'}$ and ${\\boldsymbol Y} = (Y_1, \\ldots, Y_m)^{'}$ denote two random vectors.\r\r- Consider two linear combinations of these variables as $$\\begin{aligned}\r  T & = \\sum_{i=1}^n a_iX_i\\\\\r  W & = \\sum_{i=1}^m b_iY_i\r  \\end{aligned}$$ for specified constants $a_i$ and $b_i$, $i = 1, 2, \\ldots, n$.\r\r- Supoose $E[X_i] = \\mu_{X_i}$, $i=1, 2, \\ldots, n$ and $E[Y_i] = \\mu_{Y_i}$, $i=1, 2, \\ldots, m$.\r\r### Mean and variance of $T$\r\r::: {#thm-ch2-15}\r- $E[T] = \\sum_{i=1}^n a_i \\mu_{X_i}$ and $E[W] = \\sum_{i=1}^n b_i \\mu_{Y_i}$.\r\r- $\\operatorname{Var}(T) = \\sum_{i=1}^n a_i^2\\operatorname{Var}(X_i) + 2\\sum_{i\u003cj} a_i a_j \\operatorname{Cov}(X_i, X_j)$.\r\r- $Cov(T, W) = \\sum_{i=1}^n\\sum_{j=1}^ma_ib_j\\operatorname{Cov}(X_i, Y_j)$.\r:::\r\r### Random sample\r\r::: {#def-ch2-12}\r- If the random variables $X_1, X_2, \\ldots, X_n$ are independent and identically distributed.\r\r- Each $X_i$ has the same distribution.\r\r- These random variables constitute a random sample of size $n$ from that common distribution.\r\r- Abbreviate independent and identically distributed by iid.\r:::\r\r### Example 38 （對應課本 Example 2.8.2，Sample Variance）\r\r- Let $X_i$, $i=1, 2, \\ldots, n$, be a random sample with mean $\\mu$ and variance $\\sigma^2$.\r\r- Define the sample variance as $$S^2 = \\frac{1}{n-1}\\sum_{i=1}^n (X_i-\\bar{X})^2$$\r\r- Show $E[S^2]=\\sigma^2$.\r\r[^1]: Hint: Define $h(v) = E\\left\\{[(X-\\mu_1) + v (Y-\\mu_2)]^2\\right\\}$\r\r## 本章摘要\r\r本章把單變量分配推廣到隨機向量，核心工具是邊際化、條件化、Jacobian 與共變異結構。\r\r## 常見錯誤\r\r- 將零相關直接解讀為獨立\r- Jacobian 使用方向錯誤\r- 只求聯合密度而未確認轉換後支持集\r- 忽略條件分配中的正規化常數\r\r## 章末練習\r\r1. 從本章選出三個關鍵定義，分別寫出數學形式、成立條件及一個例子。\r2. 選擇一個主要定理或方法，重做推導並標記每一步使用的假設。\r3. 從原講義例題挑選一題，完整寫出解題目標、方法選擇、計算與結果解釋。\r4. 設計一個會違反本章方法條件的反例，說明錯誤會出現在哪一步。\r\r::: {.callout-note
